INTRODUCTION 
Lifting and hoisting operations are one of the major causes of fatalities and serious incidents globally. 
Every type of lift has a set of risks that need to be managed if the lift is to be undertaken in a safe and 
efficient manner. 
 
Possible contributory reasons for accidents occurring during lifting operations are (but not limited to) 
inadequate planning of the lifting operation, inadequate control of operations, inadequate competency of 
any and all team members, operator’s error, equipment failure, etc. 
 
This Standard establishes the requirements for those involved in the supply, operation and control of 
lifting equipment, so that the risks associated with the use of lifting equipment are assessed, that control 
measures are implemented in accordance with hierarchy of controls and that control measures are taken 
to prevent injury, illness and disease to persons who might be exposed to risks arising from those 
activities and to safeguard the assets. 
 
 
PURPOSE 
The purpose of this Standard, through its implementation is to standardize the approach to management 
of lifting and hoisting operations in all ADNOC Group companies; to define lifting and hoisting operations 
process in compliance with applicable legislations and standards; to define roles and responsibilities of 
those involved; to ensure the lifting and hoisting operations are planned and conducted in a safe manner 
without undue risk to the health, safety and welfare of persons performing the lifting and hoisting operation, 
asset  and environment; to ensure that contractor's requirements for lifting and hoisting operations are 
equal or higher than those of ADNOC and to protect those who are not directly involved in the lifting and 
hoisting operation such as members of the public, visitors and representatives of local authorities to site 
including contractors and company assets. 
 
 
SCOPE 
This Standard shall apply to all ADNOC Group personnel, contractors and third parties engaged in lifting 
and hoisting operations and to all lifting equipment to be used at all ADNOC Group operating facilities, 
Major Projects, Greenfield Projects, Brownfield Projects, Drilling, Logistics, Operations, Maintenance, 
Inspection and Decommissioning both in the onshore and offshore environments. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
COVERAGE AND EXCLUSION 
3.1.1 
LIFTING EQUIPMENT CLASSIFICATION 
This Standard is applicable to all lifting equipment unless specifically excluded herein [Refer to Section 
3.1.2]. Lifting Equipment are classified into three main categories: Lifting Appliances, Lifting Accessories

5. 
DEFINITIONS & ABBREVIATIONS 
TERMS 
DESCRIPTIONS 
Active Heave 
Compensation (AHC) 
Active Heave Compensation is a system, which maintains the 
position of the load being lifted by a crane using external power 
supply alone or also on the release of stored energy 
ADNOC 
Abu Dhabi National Oil Company 
AI 
Asset Integrity 
AISC 
American Institution of Steel Construction 
ALARP 
As low as reasonably practicable 
Anomaly 
An anomaly is any condition that may impair the Lifting Equipment’s 
fitness for purpose when measured against the design intent of the 
Lifting Equipment. Anomalies may be discovered during inspection, 
monitoring, or as a result of routine operations and maintenance 
activities 
API 
American Petroleum Institute 
Appointed Person (AP) 
Person nominated by the management to be in overall control of 
lifting operations in his area, he shall be qualified, trained, certified 
and be competent to plan and supervise the specific Lifting 
Operations 
Assessment 
Process of judging competency against prescribed standards of 
performance 
Assessor 
A highly trained, experienced and competent person who makes 
judgments on the skills and knowledge of such persons like a Crane 
Operator, Rigger, Banksman, etc. The Assessor shall have sufficient 
technical and theoretical knowledge, practical experience and the 
required qualities with specific regard to Lifting and Hoisting 
Operations & Lifting Equipment 
Asset Owner 
Person responsible and accountable for the integrity and safe 
condition of the complete structure, facility, systems and equipment 
within allocated to him/her. In Operations, this person may be for 
example, site based Senior Vice President Operations or Vice 
President Operations or corresponding position 
Automatic Overload 
Protection System (AOPS) 
Automatic Overload Protection System is a system that automatically 
safeguards and protects a Crane against the effects of a gross 
overload during Lifting Operation’s by allowing the Hook to be pulled 
away from the Crane in any direction without causing significant 
damage to the Crane 
Blind Lift 
A lift where at any point in time during the lifting operation, the Crane 
Operator cannot directly see the load 
BMU 
Building Maintenance Unit 
Cargo 
Any liquid, solid or gaseous material transported in a Cargo carrying 
Unit (CCU) and items such as bundled Pipes, Tubulars etc. 
Cargo Carrying Unit (CCU)
 
 
Equipment with dedicated corner fittings used in lifting and 
transportation of materials/equipment

TERMS 
DESCRIPTIONS 
CCU Data Plate: 
Suffix T V N 
 
Suffix V 
 
Suffix V N 
 
Designates Proof Load Test, Visual & Non-destructive Examination 
undertaken 
Designates Visual Examination only undertaken 
Designates Visual & Non-destructive Examination undertaken 
Certification 
Certification is an approved and legally compliant method of checking 
and providing written evidence that a piece of equipment has been 
examined and meets required standards.  
In the context of this Standard, certification refers to the process 
whereby lifting equipment is certified by the responsible Classification 
Society, Statutory Authority or other Competent Third Party Agency 
deemed to be acceptable by ADNOC 
CF 
Consequence Factor 
CoG 
Center of Gravity 
CoG Shift Factor 
(CSF) 
The Centre of Gravity Shift Factor is used to account for additional 
loads due to possible shift in COG during lifting operations 
Color Code 
A method of marking Lifting Equipment (with paint or colored tie 
wraps) to give a visual indication of its Certification status. The color 
coding is changed periodically (6 monthly) in accordance with a Color 
Coding System 
COMAH 
Control of Major Accident Hazards 
Company 
Abu Dhabi National Oil Company 
Competent Person 
(General) 
A person who has sufficient technical and theoretical knowledge, 
practical experience and the required qualities for the task in hand. Is 
able to perform the activities within an occupation or function to the 
standard expected for undertaking the task 
Competent Person (Lifting) 
A person who has sufficient technical and theoretical knowledge, 
practical experience and the required qualities with specific regard to 
Lifting Operations & Lifting Equipment. Able to undertake inspection 
and confirm fitness for use of Lifting Equipment; ability to detect 
deficiencies and to assess their criticality in relation to strength and 
performance, and to recommend remedial action to ensure that 
conditions are appropriate for safe use; to advise in respect of the 
creation and use of Lifting Operation Plans; and appreciate the lifting 
competence regime such that they only issue Lifting Equipment to 
those persons sufficiently competent to use such Lifting Equipment 
Component 
A Component is a single part or assembly of parts of Lifting 
Equipment, which is subjected to load effects e.g. Hooks, Ropes 
(static or running), Traverse Beams, Pendant Bars, Wheels, Axles, 
Gears, Couplings, Brakes, Hoists, Hydraulic Cylinders, Shafts and 
Pins 
Component (Primary) 
A Primary Component is a component which is essential for the 
safety and structural integrity of an item of Lifting Equipment 
Component (Secondary) 
A Secondary Component is any other component that is not a 
Primary Component

TERMS 
DESCRIPTIONS 
Consequence Factor (CF) 
A factor to ensure that main structural members, lift points, lifting 
beams and spreader bars /frames have an increased factor of safety 
(including lateral loads) related to the consequence of their failure. A 
consequence factor is also used in the design of slings and grommets 
used for lifting operations 
Container (Offshore) 
An Offshore Container (or Offshore CCU) is a portable unit with a 
maximum gross mass not exceeding 25,000 Kgs. for repeated use in 
the transport of goods or equipment handled in open seas to, from 
and between fixed and/or floating installations and ships  
Contingency Factor (CTF) 
A Factor in which the Calculated Weight of Load shall be multiplied 
with in order to determine the Actual Net Weight of Load 
Contractor 
A Company contracted to undertake the works of a Project and is 
accountable to ADNOC or its nominated representative 
CoP 
Code of Practice 
Dangerous Goods 
Goods classified, and labelled, according to the International 
Maritime Dangerous Goods (IMDG) Code as hazardous to 
personnel/equipment 
DHL 
Dynamic Hook Load 
DP 
Dynamic Positioning 
Drop Zone 
It is an area at ground level in which it is foreseeable that items would 
land if they were dropped or if they were to fall from the structure 
DTI 
Dial Test Indicator 
Dynamic Amplification 
Factor (DAF) 
The factor by which the gross weight is multiplied, to account for 
accelerations and impacts during the lifting operation  
Dynamic Lift 
A crane lifting a load from or to anywhere not on the platform/vessel 
that the crane is mounted on (from/to Supply boats, for example); it 
is also applicable for transferring a load by a crawler crane 
Engineer 
A graduate engineer or preferably a Member of the Institute of 
Engineers, or recognized equivalent that is competent and has 
adequate experience to assure that the technical requirements of this 
Standard are met 
EOTC 
Electric Overhead Travelling Crane 
Examination  
(Lifting Equipment) 
Examination of Lifting Equipment is a careful and critical assessment 
of a component or components of an item of Lifting Equipment, 
carried out by a Competent Person. The Examination should include 
dismantling of the assembly and performing a visual assessment of 
the condition of each component, supplemented by other means such 
as measurement and NDE, as deemed necessary. The Examination 
is formerly recorded 
Factor of Safety (FOS) 
Factor of Safety is the ratio of the load that would cause failure of an 
item of Lifting Equipment to the load that is imposed upon it in service, 
i.e. SWL (this is to allow for detrimental criteria such as wear and tear, 
dynamic loadings etc.) 
FC 
Fiber Core

TERMS 
DESCRIPTIONS 
FIBC 
Flexible Intermediate Bulk Container 
FROG 
The FROG is the trade name for a specifically designed and 
manufactured personnel carrier/capsule that is used for crane 
assisted personnel transfers. Also known as a Personnel Carrier 
Functional Test 
Operation of each motion of a Lifting Appliance without a load being 
applied in order to determine whether the Lifting Appliance performs 
as the OEM intended. It might be necessary to include the lifting of a 
suitable load once the Lifting Appliance has been tested without a 
load applied 
Gross Weight 
The maximum permissible combined weight of a cargo container and 
its contents i.e. GROSS WEIGHT = TARE WEIGHT + PAY LOAD 
Group Company 
means any company within the ADNOC Group other than ADNOC 
Height of Lift 
Height of Lift is the maximum amount of possible vertical travel of a 
load being lifted between the top and bottom connection points e.g. 
distance from the Crane Hook to when it is at its highest point under 
the boom sheave assembly or the vertical distance between the 
ground/deck and the Crane Hook which defines the actual height the 
load is to be lifted 
HMPE 
High Modulus Polyethylene 
HOL 
Height of Lift 
HSE 
Health, Safety & Environment 
HSECES 
HSE Critical Equipment & Systems 
HSEIA 
HSE Impact Assessment 
HSEMS 
HSE Management System 
IA 
Integrity Authority 
ID No. 
A unique identifier number given to an item of (Lifting) Equipment for 
Registration/Certification purposes to facilitate traceability 
IES 
Illuminating Engineering Society 
ILO 
International Labor Organization 
IMCA 
International Marine Dangerous Goods 
IMDG 
International Maritime Dangerous Goods 
Inshore Lift 
A lift performed to or from a free floating or anchored vessel in a 
sheltered still water environment e.g. jetty lifts to/from a supply boat, 
near shore lifts vessel to vessel 
Inspection 
Inspection is defined as a detailed visual examination and other such 
measures considered necessary by an Inspector to determine the 
condition of the lifting equipment. Inspection includes visual, 
dimensional, ultrasonic, magnetic particle testing, opening up and 
dismantling or any other such means as considered necessary by the 
Inspector.  Refer to the definitions of ‘Certified visual inspection’ and 
‘Thorough visual inspection’ for application of the inspection 
measures

TERMS 
DESCRIPTIONS 
IOGP 
International Association of Oil & Gas Producers 
IVB 
Independent Verification Body  
IWRC 
Independent Wire Rope Core 
Job Safety analysis (JSA) 
A process of formal identification, recording and assessment of the 
risks involved in any particular operation so that appropriate controls 
can be introduced 
LARS 
Launching & Recovery System 
LEEA 
Lifting Equipment Engineers Association 
Lifted Equipment 
Any device that is used to suspend the load, e.g., containers, tanks, 
skips, skids, drum racks, pipe racks, frames, gas cylinder racks, 
pallets, flexible industrial bulk containers (‘big bags’), tree cages, 
cargo nets, and cargo baskets 
Lifting Accessory 
Any device that is used or designed to be used directly or indirectly 
to connect a load to a lifting appliance and does not form part of the 
load e.g., slings, hooks and fittings, swivels, hoist rings, turnbuckles, 
sheave blocks, wedge sockets, lifting harnesses, shackles, eye-bolts, 
rigging screws, wedge sockets, plate clamps, and spreader beams, 
drill pipe and casing elevators 
Lifting Appliance 
Any mechanical device capable of raising or lowering a load, eg, 
cranes, jacks, mobile aerial platforms, pad eyes, forklift trucks, 
powered hoists, manual hoists, lever hoists, beam trolleys, beam 
clamps, sheave blocks, winches, runway beams, mono-rail hoist 
Lifting Equipment 
Lifting equipment comprises of lifting appliances (equipment 
performing the lifting), lifting accessories (devices that connect the 
load to the lifting appliance), and lifted equipment 
Lifting Inspector 
The term inspector is used to describe any person carrying out 
inspection of lifting equipment. In all cases, the inspector shall have 
experience and training suitable to the inspection being performed.  
Where inspections referred to in this document require specific 
qualifications, these have been given in the appropriate section 
Lifting Supervisor 
Person nominated by the Appointed Person to implement the Lifting 
Plan in his area, he shall be qualified, trained, certified and be 
competent to supervise the specific Lifting Operations; he could 
prepare certain categories of Lifting Plans provided he is qualified, 
trained and certified to do so 
Lifting Technical Authority 
(LTA) 
Person/Team nominated by the management to ensure that all lifting 
operations including contractors at all ADNOC sites are implemented 
in proper compliance to ADNOC Lifting Standard. Reference shall be 
made to Section 6.1.1 of this standard for details 
Limit Switch 
A Limit Switch is an automatic actuated switch which is a safety

DESCRIPTIONS 
Load Chart 
A Load Chart is a diagram or table showing the Safe Working Load 
and the Rated Capacity relative to the radius, environmental 
conditions, out of plane influences and type of operation. The load 
chart shall state the in-service operational limits of the Crane, e.g.

Man-lift

Manual Overload 
Protection System (MOPS) 
Manual Overload Protection System is a system activated by the 
Crane Operator that protects the crane against possible overload by 
reducing the load carrying capacity and allowing the Hook to be 
pulled away from the Crane in any direction 
Manufacturer 
The term "Manufacturer" as used in this Standard includes all

be calculated or weighed 
Non-Destructive 
Examination/Testing (NDE/ 
NDT) 
Non-Destructive Examination/Testing is carried out on a structure to 
establish presence, location and extent of any defect indication that 
can affect the integrity of that structure 
Offshore Installation 
An Offshore Installation is a fixed structure supported by the sea bed 
or floating unit supported by buoyancy, used for the exploration, 
production and storage of hydrocarbons in a marine environment 
Onshore Lift 
A lift performed entirely on land. (e.g. from land to land) 
Original Equipment

in above) 
OSHA 
Occupational Safety & Health Administration

Abu Dhabi Occupational Safety and Health System Framework 
Overload Testing  
(Dynamic Factor) 
Operation of full range of each motion of the Lifting Appliance (as 
applicable and safe) with a load that exceeds the rated load applied 
in order to determine whether the Lifting Appliance is stable, 
structurally sound and fit for the use for which it is designed

Overload/ Proof Load

(PHC)

Is to check the equipment under inspection to perform movements 
with load to verify all movements including critical safety devices such

RACI is an acronym derived from the four key responsibilities:

RCI/ SLI 
Rated Capacity Indicator/Safe Load Indicator 
Safe Working Load (SWL) 
The maximum load (as determined by a competent person) that an 
item of lifting equipment may raise, lower or suspend under particular 
service conditions, e.g., the SWL can be lower than, but can never 
exceed, the WLL 
 
Normally SWL = WLL unless the lifting equipment has been de-rated 
Significant Wave Height 
(SWH) 
Significant Wave Height (H1/3) is the average height of the highest 
one third of the individual wave heights in a short-term constant state 
of sea, typically 3 hours 
Single Point Authority

of authority 
Skew Load Factor (SKL) 
The skew load factor (SKL) is used to take care of additional loading 
caused by equipment/fabrication tolerances and other uncertainties 
with respect to asymmetry and associated force distribution in the 
rigging arrangement 
Sling Angle 
The angle the sling makes with the Vertical 
Snatch Lift 
Lifting a load from unmoored Supply Vessel which is not DP Vessel 
SOLAS  
International Convention for the Safety of Life at Sea  
Subsea Lift 
A subsea lifting operation means installation or removal of objects 
underwater 
TA

DESCRIPTIONS 
Tailing Lift 
An Operation performed by Secondary Crane during Tandem Lift  
Tandem Lift 
A lifting operation, which involves simultaneous use of two or more 
lifting appliances, e.g. cranes, hoists, chain blocks etc. 
Tare Weight 
The weight of an empty container or the weight of a lifting beam or

Testing 
Testing in the context of inspection is that testing shall be carried out 
periodically by a competent person, in conjunction with inspection, at 
periods defined by this document such as (Load Test, NDT…etc.) 
Third Party Agency (TPA) 
Third Party Agency (TPA) is the company contracted to undertake 
the Third Party Inspection & Verification Tasks on behalf of ADNOC 
or its contractors 
Thorough Examination 
An assessment carried out by a sufficiently independent Competent 
Person (Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector) in such depth and 
details as the competent person considers necessary to enable him 
to determine whether the equipment being examined is safe to 
continue in use. This may include Visual Inspection, Dimensional 
Measurement, Functional Testing, Load Testing and Non-Destructive 
testing where deemed appropriate by the Competent Person 
Tilt Factor (TF) 
Tilt Factor (TF) is used to account for additional load that may arise 
due to the tilting of the crane hook 
Tonne (t) 
For the purposes of this standard, One Tonne shall be interpreted as 
1000 kilograms which is equal to 2204 lbs. 
TPIA 
Third Party Inspection Agency 
Vendor 
The named Manufacturer or Supplier in the ADNOC Purchase Order. 
The term “Vendor” as used herein includes all Manufacturers and 
sub-suppliers of equipment covered by this Document 
Visual Inspection 
A visual check and, where necessary, a function check of the lifting 
equipment by a competent person before each use. In determining 
the suitability and scope of the inspection, reference shall be made to 
information such as manufacturer’s instructions and relevant industry 
standards 
Working Load Limit (WLL) 
The maximum working load designed by the manufacturer. This load 
represents a force that is much less than that required to make the 
lifting equipment fail or yield 
Working Stress Design 
Design philosophy that compares stresses induced by design loads 
to allowable stresses calculated as a percentage of material yield 
stress 
Yaw Factor (YF) 
Yaw Factor (YF) is used to account for the tolerances in lift radii of 
the crane hook

6. 
ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES 
6.1. 
GENERAL 
This section establishes roles and responsibilities of personnel involved in lifting and hoisting operations 
and list their basic responsibilities and accountabilities.  
 
6.1.1 
LIFTING TECHNICAL AUTHORITY 
The Lifting Technical Authority is responsible to provide the technical overview for lifting and hoisting 
operations. The Lifting TA also establishes the system to provide the required technical expertise in 
ensuring that all lifting operations are complying with this Standard. Depending on the complexity of the 
lifting and hoisting operation, the Lifting TA may also be required to review, approve/ endorse as 
appropriate the method statements and lifting plans prior to their implementation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Note: Lifting Technical Authority (LTA) role can be established in ADNOC Group Company HQ or Site 
depending on the ADNOC Group Company size and operating areas. This role can be combined with 
“Single Point Authority” (SPA) role as mentioned in Section 6.1.3 however, an Appointed Person (AP) 
role is a must to be available in every lifting operation at site; AP shall refer to LTA or SPA for technical 
support or approval.

SINGLE POINT AUTHORITY (SPA)

SPA can be any one of the following established posts, but not limited to, i.e. Lifting Supervisor/ Crane

Manager/ Vessel Master.

6.2. 
RACI CHART 
The roles and responsibilities for all lifting activities throughout the asset/equipment lifecycle are to be 
defined, documented and communicated. The scope of lifting operations and distribution of roles and 
responsibilities are different between ADNOC Group Companies. If the Group Company has more 
complicated or elaborated lifting operations, they could split the above stated standard roles and 
responsibilities and map it against the specific positions in the Group Company. 
 
 
REQUIREMENTS 
 
GENERAL REQUIREMENTS 
 
During the planning of lifting operations, consideration shall be given to the type of equipment that 
is required to carry out the lifting operation safely. The appointed person shall be consulted during 
the selection process and the decision on the type of crane shall be based on Weight of load, 
frequency and duration of the work; the working environment (ground conditions, access 
restrictions etc.); mobility of the crane (Will lifting operation be carried out either at one place or 
several different locations) and Lifting radius. 
 
Safe working Load (SWL) shall be clearly marked on the lifting appliance in a prominent position. 
SWL of every lifting accessory shall be clearly marked on the lifting accessory or on a metal tag 
fixed securely to the lifting accessory. Every crane with a SWL of 1 tonne or more shall be fitted 
with a Rated capacity indicator/ safe load indicator which emit an audible warning when the crane 
approaches its SWL (Ref. OSHAD-SF Cop-34). 
 
An anemometer shall be available on each worksite, where cranes are used, to measure the wind 
speed. This shall be fitted to the highest point where reasonably practicable. Handheld 
anemometers shall only be used as a secondary means of measuring wind speed at ground level. 
Provisions shall also be in place to determine the wind direction and regular meteorological reports 
(at least daily) shall be obtained for the area where cranes are being used for lifting operations.

## Page 23

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
 
 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 23 of 301 
 
Ensure that there is an agreed and recognized means of communication between the operator of 
the lifting equipment and the banksman. The agreed means of communication shall be 
documented in the lifting plan and followed by all those involved in lifting operations. 
 
Where hand signals are used (Refer to Appendix 1) as the means of communication, they shall 
be common for particular site and uniformly understood by all involved in control of lifts. The 
banksman shall be within easy viewing distance of the crane operator. The banksman shall be 
clearly identified, preferably by distinctive clothing being brightly colored and for exclusive use of 
the banksman. Where there is no clear line of site between the signaler and the crane operator, 
radio communication shall be used. 
 
On Tower cranes where the operator’s cab is more than 35 meters from the ground level, radio 
communication shall be the mandatory means of communication between the crane operator and 
the banksman (Ref. OSHAD-SF Cop-34). 
 
Blind lifts should be avoided as much as reasonably possible. All laydown, storage and lifting areas 
should be planned with no blind zones and thus minimizing the need for blind lifts. But in the case 
where blind lifts are unavoidable, communications should be given the highest attention during the 
risk assessment. 
 
In connection with blind lifts, there shall always be at least two persons (Banksman and Rigger) 
who have visual contact with the load and each other; and have radio contact with the lifting 
appliance operator. Any closed-circuit television that monitors the work area is considered to be 
an aid and not a replacement for either of these persons. 
 
The banksman shall place himself in a position where he has clear visual contact with the lifting 
appliance operator and can give the stop signal manually in the event of radio equipment failure. 
Communication from the banksman to the crane operator should be continuous, two way and 
confirmative by repeating the command. This is particularly important during “blind lifts”. 
 
PLANNING THE LIFTING AND HOISTING OPERATIONS  
7.2.1 
GENERAL 
All lifting operations shall be properly planned, appropriately supervised and carried out to protect the 
safety of workers. This section outlines the process for planning the lifting and hoisting operations that is 
applicable to all lifting operations across ADNOC Group, including contracted work.  
 
 
The Asset Owner should identify or be informed of the need for a lifting and hoisting operation and 
designate an AP. The AP shall plan and, if appropriate, supervise the lifting and hoisting operation. 
 
Every lifting and hoisting operation shall be risk assessed and planned by the AP before the work 
begins. The AP shall ensure that appropriate controls are in place for those hazards identified in 
the written risk assessment such that the risks are managed as an integral part of the “Lifting Plan”. 
 
 
The Appointed Person shall begin the planning process by determining the scope of work ‐ defining 
what is to be done, who shall be involved, how the lifting and hoisting operations will be conducted. 
This may require an initial “Lifting Plan”, sketches, notes etc. If additional technical support is 
required, it shall be obtained before proceeding.

Having identified a requirement for the lifting and hoisting operation it shall be established if the 
particular operation has been carried out before on that site, and a Risk Assessment and “Lifting 
Plan” may already exist. If a particular Risk Assessment and “Lifting Plan” do exist, then they shall 
be assessed to confirm that they are still applicable. Changes to personnel, site layout or work 
environment could all result in the need for a reassessment of hazards. Assuming that significant 
changes have not taken place, lifting and hoisting operation may proceed under the requirements 
of the “Lifting Plan”, subject to the normal approvals. If the lifting and hoisting operation has not 
been carried out before then a Risk Assessment shall be carried out and a “Lifting Plan” prepared. 
 
 
7.2.2 
LIFTING OPERATION PROCESSS  
This section defines the process that all lifting operations shall comply with, shown schematically in below 
figure.  A Lifting Plan (Refer to Appendix 10) and Pre-Lift/Pre-use Checklist (Refer to Appendix 14) 
shall be completed and approved prior to all lifts to assure that lifting operations are executed safely by 
competent personnel, utilizing safe equipment and under appropriate supervision.

CATEGORIZING THE LIFTING OPERATION

applicable controls and personnel requirement are specified hereunder.

The Appointed Person or principal contractor’s representative shall normally take the opportunity during 
the site induction to seek the views of the team about any arrangements for health and safety that might 
be relevant to them. 
To be effective, the method statement needs to specify clear roles for each member of the team. 
Arrangements for effective communication among the team (and if necessary, with adjacent crane 
operators in case of danger) shall be available. 
 
7.2.5 
RISK ASSESSMENT 
The risk assessment of all foreseeable hazards and their associated risks for all lifting activities across 
ADNOC Group, including contracted work shall be conducted in line with the requirements of ADNOC 
standard for JSA. 
 
The results of the risk assessment shall be recorded in writing and used in the preparation of the Lifting 
Plan (Refer to Section 7.2.6 below) for that site. 
 
The Risk Assessment shall address, but is not limited to, the following risks and hazards: 
 
Hoisting over live equipment; 
 
Number and duration of lifts; 
 
Hoisting of personnel; 
 
Conflicting tasks in area; 
 
Environmental conditions, including weather and permissible limits; 
 
Lighting in the pick‐up and lay down areas; 
 
Proximity hazards, obstructions, path of load; 
 
Working at height; 
 
Working under suspended loads, which is never allowed; 
 
Access and emergency escape routes for all members of the lifting and hoisting team; 
 
Visibility of the load by the operator and/or banksman; and 
 
Communication between all personnel taking part in the lifting and hoisting operation. 
 
Adverse weather conditions will increase the risks presented to personnel, equipment and assets 
as a result of lifting and hoisting operations. Prior to commencing hoisting operations, the crane 
operator shall take stock of the prevailing weather conditions (wind speed and direction, visibility, 
etc.). Lifting and hoisting operations shall not commence, where, in the crane operator/ shift 
controller’s opinion, the safety of personnel, plant or equipment may be jeopardized.

## Page 34

HSE Management System

Effective date: August 2019 
Page 34 of 301 
When lifting operations have been temporarily suspended due to inclement weather, or in case of 
supply vessel back loading/discharging because of the prevailing sea-state, resuming of suspended 
lifting operation shall be controlled as per PTW/ TRA requirements.  
 
 
The weather criteria for Onshore Crane Operations  
The weather criteria for all Onshore Cranes operations shall be as follows: 
Maximum wind speed shall not be more than 20 knots (10 m/sec.) – (Ref. OSHAD-SF; CoP-34.0) 
steady wind measured at 10 meters above the sea level for Materials Lifting and 15 knots (7 m/sec.) 
for Personnel Lifting, measured with a calibrated hand-held anemometer at a similar level to that to 
which the carrier will be lifted or as per the Crane Manufacturer’s Wind Speed Limit whichever is 
the lesser- (Ref. BS 7121-3:2017). 
 
 
The weather criteria for Offshore Crane Operations 
The weather criteria for all Offshore Cranes operations shall be as follows: 
Maximum wind speed shall not be more than 25 Knots (12 m/ sec.) and Significant Wave Height 
(SWH) 2 m for material lifting and 20 knots (10 m/ Sec.) and Significant Wave Height 2 m (Ref. BS 
EN 13852) for Personnel Lifting or as per the Crane Manufacturer’s Wind Speed Limit whichever is 
the lesser. 
 
 
Rainfall & Thunderstorm 
If the lifting operations and personnel transfer is proposed during a period of rainfall or Thunderstorm, 
consideration shall be given to the severity of the weather, and the lifting operations and personnel 
transfer postponed to more favorable conditions if there is any concern that it could affect the 
operations. 
Note: In the case manufacturer specifications allow operations at higher wind speeds then 
operations for lifting/hoisting of material shall be only carried out under the category of complex lift 
as specified in this standard with proper risk assessment taking into account the Load chart and 
also the effect of ambient wind on the load & crane stability, but shall not exceed the manufacturer’s 
specifications at any point. 
 
Lifting operations and personnel transfer operations shall only be carried out during daylight hours. 
In case, the lifting operations are unavoidable, the provision of adequate lightings and searchlights 
(Minimum 100 lux as per IES Standard) shall be made, to enable the crane operator to have 
adequate visibility throughout lifting operations and landing areas. The Personnel being transferred 
shall wear the clothing with reflective tape and recovery arrangements shall be addressed.  
 
In extreme conditions, crane operations shall be stopped until there is sufficient improvement in 
visibility to enable operations to be resumed safely. Where a crane is mounted on a vessel and 
subject to adverse sea states, account should be taken of the angles of heel being experienced and 
of the vessel stability. A risk assessment shall be performed that shows that the risks are as low as 
reasonably practicable (ALARP). 
 
Note: Personnel transfer operations shall only be carried out during daylight hours and it is not 
allowed during poor visibility unless it is an emergency. 
 
 
Cranes shall always be adequately supported to ensure the lift is safe. On site, competent personnel 
shall be contacted to ensure there are no potential problems at the location where the crane will be 
working. 
 
For heavier lifts, the ultimate bearing capacity of the ground can be approached, even with the use 
of crane mats to spread out the track or outrigger loads.

Potential hazards can arise where a crane is positioned over an underground cavity e.g. piping 
ducts, buried water pipes, electrical conduits/cables, liquid and gas pipes, or sewers.  Collapse of 
the cavity while the crane is lifting can lead to catastrophic failure.  Possible engineering measures 
to protect the cavities include compacted fill, or protective slabs. 
 
Possible failure conditions shall still be considered when setting up a crane on engineered surfaces 
such as concrete paving, concrete ground floor slabs or suspended slab. For example, if concrete 
paving has been placed on poorly compacted fill the fill can settle leaving a void, this can result in 
the outrigger punching through the paving. Similarly, the weight of the outrigger may exceed the 
ultimate load on a concrete slab leading to punch through. 
 
Reference shall be made to below Appendix 9 (Lifting Plan Dossier) and Appendix 11 (Lifting Data 
Table-Onshore Cranes) for Crane’s Outrigger/Tracks Pressure on Ground in comparison with Site 
Ultimate Ground Bearing Pressure considering the applicable Safety Factor. 
 
 
The lifting radius for a crane is the horizontal distance from the crane's vertical axis of rotation to the 
centre of gravity of the (freely suspended) load. 
The term “Outreach” is normally used in the Derrick Cranes (Sheerleg) that do not revolve. The 
Outreach is measured from the Barge edge to the Load Centre of Gravity.  
The term “Outreach” is also used in Mobile Cranes for the selection of suitable crane type and 
capacity to avoid the Load striking the crane’s body. 
When initially planning a non-routine lift, an accurate plot plan of the area is invaluable in 
determining the radius. The planned radius can then be verified on an elevation view drawing when 
clearances are checked. Verification of the crane's working radius by measurement shall be 
checked against the approved lifting plan and recorded at site, before lifting commences.

Tilt Factor (TF);

7.3.2 
PERSONNEL INVOLVED IN LIFTING OPERATION 
All ADNOC Group Assets’ owner/Contractors shall ensure that all personnel involved in the inspection of 
equipment, Planning, Risk Assessment, approval and execution of lifting operations are suitably qualified 
and conform to the competence requirements set out (Refer to Section 0). 
7.3.3 
TOOL BOX TALK 
A Tool box talk shall be held to ensure that all personnel involved in the lifting operation fully understand 
the Lifting Plan. Prior to all lifts (Routine Lifts and Non-Routine Lifts) the Appointed Person shall verify that 
the answers to the following ‘Ten questions for a safe lift’ are all addressed. 
 
 
Is everyone aware of and do they fully understand the lifting and hoisting procedures 
applicable to the lift? 
 
Has everyone involved with this lifting operation attended the toolbox talk? 
 
Has a pre-use inspection of the lifting equipment been carried out and are the lifting 
accessories tagged or marked with: 
1. Safe working load? 
2. A unique identification number? 
3. Is correct color code applied and certificate validity verified? 
(iv) 
Are all safety devices working? 
(v) 
Does everyone know the Appointed Person of the lift? 
(vi) 
Is everyone competent and aware of his or her tasks? 
(vii) 
Is there a current Lifting Plan and JSA and does everybody understand the job and 
precautions? 
(viii) Does everyone know the environmental limits (e.g. maximum permissible wind speed) for the 
lift? 
(ix) 
Is the lift area controlled and is everyone clear if the load falls or swings? 
(x) 
Are signalling methods and communication agreed and clear to you?

7.4. 
SPECIAL LIFTING OPERATION 
7.4.1. NON-STANDARD STATIONARY EQUIPMENT LIFTING POINTS IN THE PLANT 
(a) 
Stationary equipment with substandard Lifting Points which do not comply with any current 
International relevant Standard shall be thoroughly examined. 
 
(b) 
Structural Engineer shall review the drawings to confirm the suitability of the lifting points to be used 
and to confirm there has been no modification carried out after the equipment installation at site. 
The Site Structural Inspector shall physically check the Lifting Points and confirm the current 
condition is acceptable. 
 
(c) 
Non-Destructive Testing (NDT) shall be carried out on the Lifting Points. ADNOC Group approved 
Third Party using approved Lifting Equipment Inspector shall review NDT reports and related 
drawings before proceeding with thorough examination and corresponding details shall be clearly 
mentioned in the certificate. 
7.4.2. CRAWLER CRANE SET-UP ONBOARD OF FLOATING VESSEL/BARGE

## Page 40

HSE Management System

7.4.3. DYNAMIC LIFTING OPERATIONS 
A Dynamic Lift happens, when a cargo is transferred from the moving Supply Vessel Deck to a Fixed 
Installation Deck, especially in inclement weather / higher sea states, when the hoisting motion starts to 
lift a cargo, and vessel drops into the trough of a wave. The resulting shock load will create a dynamic 
effect that can increase the load on the crane by up to three times the actual weight being lifted. This effect 
will continue for a short time, depending on the boom length and angle. Unless the risk of dynamic loading 
is addressed, this dynamic loading will result in a series of overloading peaks applied to the Crane.  
To alleviate dynamic loading effect the Crane Operator should hoist the Cargo from the Vessel Deck just 
as the Supply Vessel is rising or is at the top of the wave. More importantly, competent ADNOC Group 
approved crane operator shall ensure correct selection and setting of the appropriate Sea State on the 
Rated Capacity Indicator (RCI). Refer to Appendix 8 for more details. Sea State Duties will de-rate the 
crane’s Safe Working Load (SWL), depending on the corresponding Significant Wave Height (SWH). This 
reduction in capacity is an allowance for the dynamic effect; an increase between actual load moment and 
peak load moment. Proper care shall be taken at all times during dynamic lifting operations to avoid the 
hazards within the area of operation. 
 
7.4.4. LOADING/UNLOADING OF SUPPLY VESSELS 
The Vessel Master is responsible for the safety of the crew and vessel at all times and has authority to 
decide whether operations affecting the vessel shall proceed or be terminated. 
The Vessel Master is responsible for the safe and correct loading of his vessel. He shall liaise with the 
Logistics Service Provider to ensure that the vessel is loaded correctly and in accordance with ADNOC 
specific requirements. The Master shall ensure that the deck area occupied by cargo does not exceed 
agreed utilization factors. Due consideration shall be given to known discharge priorities for the cargo on 
that voyage in order to avoid “Cherry Picking”. 
In conjunction with the base operator, vessel deck crew shall ensure the lifting equipment is readily 
available for handling at point of discharge, with particular attention to half height open top containers. 
The vessel deck crew shall make a final visual check to ensure the lift is safe prior to the hook being 
attached to the load for discharge offshore. 
The Logistics Service Provider or materials coordinator shall provide the vessel with a copy of the loading 
list complete with all relevant Dangerous Goods information for each installation to be visited and an 
accurate manifest. These documents shall be received in sufficient time to permit proper stowage of the 
cargo for the route envisaged. 
7.4.5. TUBULARS 
This category includes items, such as drill pipe, risers, conductor, casing, tubing, scaffolding materials 
and other pipelines. 
(a) 
General 
The following applies to both individual and bundled tubular:

## Page 41

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 41 of 301 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
(b) 
Individually Slung Tubulars 
Prior to loading individual tubular cargo, bedding rope shall be placed at appropriate positions on 
the vessels intended loading area. The minimum of two certified securing arrangements shall be 
placed at equal distance approximately 25% from the ends of the intended stow. The length and/or 
height of securing arrangements shall be sufficient to ensure that the entire tubular stow is secured. 
Examples of certified securing arrangements are lashing chain, webbing, wire, pipe pins/stanchions, 
stretchers, ratchets, shackles, etc. 
Specifically for vessel loading, only tubulars of the same diameter are to be stowed together and 
wherever possible should have similar length to ensure the tubulars are properly secured and 
positioned between the securing arrangements. Smaller individual joints or pup joints that cannot 
be stowed between securing arrangements and are considered to be less than 60% overall length 
of average joint, shall be secured as a separate item or shipped in cargo baskets. 
(c) 
Bundled Tubulars 
Certified transportation frames are considered best practice for smaller dimension tubulars. Only 
tubulars of the same diameter shall be bundled together and whenever possible should be of similar 
length. The number of tubulars in each bundle shall be such that the inside/ middle tubulars are 
gripped and will not slip out of the bundle. Whenever practicable, tubulars over 5.5” in diameter 
should be bundled in ‘odd’ numbers. 
7.4.6. PORTABLE GAS EQUIPMENT/TANKS/CYLINDERS/RACKS 
(a) 
General

## Page 42

Gas products are used in various forms at all ADNOC installations. They are typically transported 
within portable gas equipment such as tanks and cylinders.  Although cylinders & tanks in lift frames/ 
racks are also widely used. Such equipment is subject to the certification and testing requirements 
as specified in Section 7 of Appendix 5. 
All companies providing gas in portable gas equipment to contractors, owners and operators of 
ADNOC installations, and all ADNOC installations returning transportable gas containers shall 
ensure that: 
 Suitable cylinder types are used; 
 Portable gas equipment is checked prior to shipment; and  
 Specific requirements are met for Gas Quads/Packs/Multiple Element Gas Containers (MEGCs) 
and Lift Frames 
(b) 
Cylinder Types 
Equipment used for the transportation of dangerous goods shall be designed, constructed, 
inspected and tested in accordance with the provisions of the current IMDG Code, the Carriage of 
Dangerous Goods and Use of Transportable Pressure Equipment Regulations [SI 2007/ 1573], 
ADNOC HSE Standard HSE-OS-ST07 and any other appropriate standards (i.e. BS EN 1968, BS 
EN ISO 10855, DNVGL-ST-E271 and ABS Guide for of Offshore Containers). 
(c) 
General Checks for Portable Gas Equipment 
The companies, providing gas products in transportable equipment for owners and operators of 
ADNOC installations and all ADNOC installations returning products and waste back shall ensure 
that their pre-dispatch checks take account of equipment integrity. 
Reference shall also be made to ADNOC HSE Standard HSE-OS-ST07 for Compressed Gas 
Cylinders.  
(d) 
Specific Requirements for Gas Quads/Packs/MEGCs (Multiple Element Gas Container) 
 
Ensure that valves and manifolds are suitably protected with valve guarding mesh and valve 
guarding mesh is secure and in good condition; 
 
Ensure that all valves are in good condition and operate correctly; 
 
All cylinder valves and king (main) valves are fully closed; 
 
Ensure that manifold is free from damage;  
 
All cylinders shall be properly secured in frame. 
 
Specific Requirements for Lift Frames; 
 
Ensure that door is in good condition and operates correctly; 
 
Where fitted, ensure that the door-securing retaining pin is engaged; 
 
Ensure equipment identification number is painted on roof; 
 
Ensure cylinders are properly restrained within lift frame to prevent movement during transit; 
and 
 
Ensure positive security of cylinders in rack by strapping, of serviceable condition, is in place. 
Small cylinders may require additional packing and/or a special container. Bottles shipped in 
racks without valve protection plates shall be capped.

All cargo in this category shall be considered as non-routine and the lifting operations planned 
accordingly. It is strongly recommended that, when planning the shipment of special cargo, logistics 
personnel are involved at the earliest opportunity. 
 
 
The factors shall be taken into account when assessing unusual items of cargo are as follows: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Because of the fragile nature and the high value of these items, they shall be transported in a 
specially designed lifting frame/ module. If not, the following points shall be considered: 
 
 
These items shall be crated in a heavy-duty material to reduce the potential for damage;  
1. The crate shall also be made accordingly and enable to be lifted from a CCU. The crate 
shall come complete with its own lifting arrangements; 
2. The crate shall be labelled to clearly identify the fragile nature of the goods;

## Page 44

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards

Effective date: August 2019 
Page 44 of 301 
3. The crate shall then be loaded into a container ensuring due consideration has been 
taken for removal when it reaches its final destination. The item shall then be secured in 
the container as detailed within these guidelines; 
4. Consideration shall be given to keep items dry; and  
5. Special Lifts to Normally Unattended Installations. 
Some NUIs (Normally Unattended Installations) have crane weight restrictions, which mean that 
placing even the smallest of equipment in a CCU will lead to the SWL of the crane being exceeded. 
Where such crane weight restrictions apply, operations shall be risk assessed, with the following 
point being considered: 
 
It may be safer to use fiber slings or suitably coated wire rope slings to lift equipment or place 
equipment inside and remove from CCUs; and  
 
In the case of scaffolding, if it cannot be loaded into a half height container due to crane weight 
restrictions then it shall be pre-slung and secured using steel banding or other suitable 
alternatives around the bundle to prevent movement. 
 
Vessels supplying the offshore industry are required to carry a variety of bulk liquids in dedicated 
tanks within the ship to supply installations offshore. It has been noted on a number of occasions 
that bulk liquids back loaded to ships have been incorrectly declared by offshore installations and 
drilling rigs and these products can have the potential to seriously impact the technical integrity of 
the vessel. 
For specific bulk backload procedures refer to Oil & Gas UK Offshore Support Vessel Guidelines. 
Particular attention is drawn to MGN 283 (M) (Marine Guidance Note). 
Examples of Special Cargo are Membrane Filter Module, Blow out Preventer (BOP), Lifeboat in 
Transportation Frame, Coiled Tubing/Cable Reel etc. 
7.4.8. TOTE TANKS 
(a) 
General 
 
Containerizing Tote Tanks with suitable CCU’s (Half-height Containers, Baskets…etc.) prior 
to dispatch them is the best industry practice; however due to shortage of CCU’s and limited 
storage areas at ADNOC sites/installations, alternative methods can be accepted subject to 
close supervision by competent personnel, proper inspection, generic risk assessment and 
generic Lifting Planning; 
 
Precautions to be taken while lifting tanks with liquids that may cause load instability due to 
free surface effect on centre of gravity. Lifting Tote Tanks using self-balancing Pallet Fork 
(Refer to Section 7.12; Appendix 5) is an alternative method in case suitable CCU is not 
available. Tote Tanks shall be secured by means of secondary load holding device, such as 
Chain, Straps or Bar capable of holding a uniformly distributed load equal to 100% of the 
Pallet Forks WLL in all four horizontal directions to prevent the load sliding off the forks. 
 
Liquid Chemicals Tote Tanks estimated with 1 t Safe Working Load (SWL) shall be lifted by 
Pallet Forks with minimum 2 t WLL (2 times SWL); and 
 
Handling Tote Tanks shall only by using suitable Forklift Trucks. 
(b) 
Transportation 
Chemicals transported by Tote Tanks and without dedicated CCUs shall be lashed or wedged to 
avoid any movement and possible damage and spillage of Chemicals.  
7.4.9. LOOSE CARGO 
All other loose cargo not mentioned here shall be containerized in suitable CCUs prior to shipment.

7.4.10. HANDLING, TRANSPORTATION AND LIFTING OF FIBC 
(a) 
GENERAL 
FIBC handling practices shall be followed at all ADNOC Group facilities, including the following:  
Logistic base to supply boat, boat to Islands, Island to jack up barge, boat to jack up barge and boat 
to offshore platforms. 
Flexible Intermediate Bulk Container is an industrial container having the body made of flexible 
material such as woven fabric, plastic film or paper, designed to be in contact with the contents, 
either directly or through an inner liner and collapsible when empty. 
Standard-duty reusable FIBCs are most often, made of polyolefin fabric, coated or uncoated, with 
or without an inner liner of plastic film and used mainly in closed loop between filler and discharge 
of the FIBC. 
ADNOC Group shall accept FIBC, designed as per International Standard BS EN ISO 21898 and 
intended to be used for one filling only and discharges as per the manufacturer’s recommendation, 
having a Safety Factor of 6:1. One number of filling is referred to FIBC’s that have reached the 
destination and have been emptied for the first time, which is not allowed to be re-used. If this 
category of FIBC has not been transferred for a period 6 months, the transfer shall be subject to 
verification by the Competent Person and Task Risk Assessment to ensure that the integrity of the 
FIBCs are maintained. Refer to Appendix 15 for FIBCs verification check list 
 
(b) 
Handling of FIBCs 
(i) 
General 
It is highly recommended to transfer FIBCs securely contained in CCUs designed as per BS 
EN ISO 10855 or DNVGL-ST-E271 or ABS Guide for Containers. However, if containerization 
is not possible, it is also acceptable to lift the FIBCs by using certified 4 leg wire rope slings 
connecting each leg individually to each FIBC strap or standard designed fabricated cross 
beams subject to Task Risk Assessment by stake holders. (Please refer to the attached 
photos). 
The following instructions shall be followed: 
1. Documents related to each shipment of FIBC shall be verified before any lift. Confirm that 
FIBCs are marked with a unique identification number, batch number, SWL, and have a 
valid certificate from the manufacturer; 
2. Any FIBC stored more than 6 months shall be verified by the Competent Person. Task 
Risk Assessment shall be carried to ensure that the integrity of the FIBC’s are maintained; 
3. Company shall reject any FIBC’s which has exceeded 6 months from date of packing, 
unless shelf life of the product is more than 6 months and FIBC checklist has been 
completed to satisfaction; 
4. Each FIBC is used for one trip only and re-use of FIBC is prevented by cutting the lifting 
straps as soon as the FIBC reaches its final destination and emptied; 
5. FIBCs located in CCUs which have side doors shall be removed with aid of the forklift. 
6. FIBCs located in CCUs which do not have side doors and cannot be removed by forklift 
will be lifted vertically from above by crane, after determining the safe lifting condition of 
the FIBCs; 
7. Inspect the hooks, bars or forklift arms used for lifting to ensure that they have rounded 
edges with a radius greater than the diameter or thickness of the suspension of the FIBCs

## Page 46

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 46 of 301 
and/or be protected by wrapping (the radius shall be minimum of 5 mm). Also, it is 
recommended to have extra packing while lifting with Forklift; 
8. Material placed in the FIBC is to be of light weight and shall not contain sharp material or 
components that could puncture or cut the FIBC; and  
9. The FIBC shall not be overfilled; sufficient room shall be left at the top to ensure that each 
FIBC is completely closed for shipment. 
 
Note: Color coding is not applicable. 
(ii) 
HANDLING FIBCs WITH CRANE 
 
 
 
(iii) 
HANDLING FIBC WITH FORKLIFT 
1. Moving FIBCs from one location to another should be carried out by using a forklift; 
2. Use a forklift truck with rated capacity sufficient to support the filled FIBC; 
3. Confirm the forklift tines used for lifting are free from sharp edges or protrusions. The 
tines shall be of adequate size with edges rounded to at least the thickness of the lift 
loops, sleeves, etc. (the radius shall be minimum of 5 mm); 
4. Confirm the distance between the forklift tines is adjusted to the correct width for the FIBC 
being handled; 
5. Confirm all lift loops or sleeves are vertical to prevent damage by lateral forces; 
6. Hold the FIBC close to the forklift mast, as low as possible with the mast tilted back to an 
appropriate angle; 
7. Confirm the FIBC is clear of the floor and not in contact with the wheels of the forklift 
8. Confirm there is a clear line of site before moving a FIBC with forklift; and  
9. Stop the forklift before raising or lowering the FIBC; 
(iv) 
STORAGE AND TRANSPORTATION OF FILLED FIBCs 
1. Confirm all FIBCs are stable when stacking; 
2. Confirm FIBCs are properly stacked in upright position (Maximum two rows in height); 
3. Confirm access around stacked materials is maintained (Drop Zone distance at least 
twice the height of the stack); 
4. Avoid subjecting a FIBC to sudden lifts or sudden stops during transportation; 
5. Confirm FIBCs are adequately secured during transportation; 
6. Confirm FIBC manufacturer’s recommendations and any instructions printed on the label 
of FIBC are followed; 
7. Confirm FIBCs are stored in a safe, clean facility or warehouse which will protect them 
from UV rays of sunlight, moisture and inclement weather; and  
8. Cover any FIBCs that would be exposed to dirt, dust or moisture inside a facility or 
warehouse.

7.4.11. LIFTING LOAD BY SCAFFOLDNIG LIFTING FRAME 
 
Hoists, winches and other lifting appliances may be mounted on scaffolding only if the scaffold 
framework is adequate in strength or is specially strengthened and tied back to reduce vibration and 
whip. The strengthening shall be calculated with reference to an effective static load of not less than 
two times the safe working load of the lifting appliance. This section defines the roles and 
responsibility of utilizing scaffolding lifting frame by attaching hoists, winches and other lifting 
appliances to scaffolding structure to raise and lower loads.  Scaffolding frame will be applied when 
there is no access for standard lifting equipment or no other alternative. 
 
        Image Courtesy: FormPak, Inc., St. Louis 
           Image Courtesy: Hawk Lifting, U.K.

The following criteria shall be applicable for the scaffolding structure which can take the imposed 
loads: 
 
Lifting frame capacity 2000 kg and below: shall be inspected by scaffolding inspector and 
load tested by scaffolding supervisor, before performing the load test, a load test procedure 
shall be identified; 
 
Lifting frame capacity above 2000 kg and below 4000 kg: Same as above plus presence of 
TPIA Representative and issuance of inspection certificate; and  
 
Lifting frame capacity above 4000 kg: shall be designed and approved by Structural Engineer. 
Scaffold structure being used as a hoisting tower, the following shall be considered: 
 
Pipe - clamp scaffold to be used, and not the prefabricated systems; 
 
All pipes & steel fittings shall be in accordance to BS 1139/EN 74; and  
 
For horizontal pipe, it will be depending upon the span and the physical properties. 
 
The following control measures shall be considered for building lifting frame under the full 
responsibilities of lifting engineer and Scaffolding supervisor. 
 
Permit to Work (PTW) is required for scaffolding erection and load testing; 
 
Lifting frame with a correct tag shall only be used; 
 
Authorized personnel are only allowed to utilize scaffolding lifting frame; 
 
In case of any modification of the scaffolding lifting frame, load test/inspection shall be 
repeated; 
 
Lifting frame shall be used only for vertical lift; Side lifting/dragging load is prohibited while 
using scaffolding lifting frame; 
 
While lifting and load testing, scaffolding stand by team is required; and  
 
Scaffolding to be used for intended purpose and upon completion of the job, tag to be 
replaced (DO NOT USE) and dismantled. 
 
 
Lifting team will define the required scaffolding frame (location and SWL). Competent 
Scaffolder will erect the scaffolding lifting frame. Scaffolding structure shall be made stable 
enough to compensate for the weight of the intended loads. Later, erected scaffolding 
structure shall be inspected by Scaffolding supervisor/competent Lifting Equipment Inspector; 
and  
 
After the load testing, scaffolding lifting frame shall be tagged (TESTED & READY TO USE). 
 
 
Check that standards are correctly aligned and properly supported at their bases and there 
is no undue deflection in ledgers and transoms; 
 
Check that all essential members of the structure are present, and all ties and braces are 
effective in stabilizing the scaffold; 
 
Check that all couplers are properly tightened, and all boards are in sound condition, correctly 
supported and, where appropriate, tied down; 
 
Check that all guardrails and toe boards are in place; 
 
Checking that all ladders are in good condition and properly secured;

Ensure that signing the SCAFFTAG, ensuring that it is properly displayed with the appropriate

Check that appropriate documentation is in place. Ensure that each scaffold and scaffold

7.4.12. SKIDDING OPERATIONS USING SKATES & GROUND TROLLEYS 
 
Care shall be taken as mentioned below when Skates and ground trolleys are used for slight 
hoisting/jacking-up and transporting materials/loads for a short distance by means of pushing/ 
pulling: 
 
Use suitable devices as recommended by the manufacturer. 
 
The Skidding operation shall be well planned; 
 
The exact Load Weight shall be either weighed or calculated; 
 
Company Structural Engineer shall be consulted and/or Structural Inspector shall verify the 
suitability of the deck/ground to which the materials/loads will be transported on; 
 
Company Structural Engineer shall be consulted and/or Structural Inspector shall verify the 
suitability of the existing structure to be used to pull/push the materials/loads; and  
 
All pulling equipment including accessories shall be inspected and certified by an ADNOC 
Group approved Lifting Equipment Inspector working in approved TPIA in UAE. 
 
Refer to Inspection Legislation Matrix in Appendix 12. 
 
Ensure that the skates and ground trolleys are marked with a unique identification number/ 
Asset Number; SWL, color code and have valid certificate of inspection from an ADNOC 
approved Third Party Company using approved Lifting Equipment Inspector; 
 
Check bearing stubs and bearings for evidence of wear. (If contaminants penetrate the 
bearing seals, rapid wear and/or seizure will result); 
 
Check for freedom of operation. Check rollers/chains for free running and for any wear at 
outside diameters; 
 
Check also for the possibility of incorrectly substituted components. (Someone may have tried 
to make one good trolley from two unserviceable trolleys); 
 
Check the rollers are free from any embedded debris before use;

7.4.13. LIFTING AND TRANSFERRING THE LOAD BY WOOD CRATES 
 
Wood crates that are not designed for lifting operations, shall not be used for general lifts. In cases 
where lifting and transferring by wood crates is unavoidable the same shall be carried out under the 
supervision with specific Lifting Plan in place. 
Standard packing crates that are packed within offshore basket or container (CCU) can only be lifted 
from the offshore basket or container (CCU) in the immediate vicinity of the main deck laydown area 
for the sole purpose of removal of the crate from the offshore basket or container (CCU). Lifting by 
slinging method shall be restricted to a minimal height of lift (HOL) clearance from the CCU and 
minimal distance of travel away from the CCU and such a lift shall always remain within the confines 
or perimeter of the main deck laydown area. Thereafter, a standard packing crate shall only then be 
transported by means of forklift to the other platform areas and only where the competent lifting 
person/Forklift handler deems the crate in a ‘Fit condition for such further transportation handling’. 
Crates that are designed for lifting may be handled in a variety of ways, but the most important from 
the standpoint of design are end slinging, forklift handling. 
Prior to lift and transfer of load onshore and offshore following steps shall be followed:  
 
A wood crate shall be designed and marked as per BS 1133, BS EN ISO 780, BS EN 20780; 
 
A wood crate shall be strong enough to protect its contents from the hazards of shipping and 
storage; 
 
Ensure that a sheathed crate with a waterproof top is advisable for shipment on deck; 
 
Ensure correct nailing of the crate panels as they are fabricated and using enough fastenings 
in assembling panels into a crate; 
 
Ensure that the base shall be skid type with flat base; 
 
Ensure that the end cross members of the base of the crate. Headers are bolted to the skids 
and act as fastening members for assembly of the end panels. They may also be used as 
reinforcing to resist forklift handling stresses at the ends; 
 
Ensure that a small opening built into the end panel to provide access for inspection of the 
contents. They can be locked or sealed; 
 Load-supporting members of the top, spanning the width of the crate. They transfer loads to 
the sides and prevent the top from being crushed when the crate is handled with slings; 
 
Ensure that rubbing strips of wood, usually 2 inches thick, nailed to the underside of each 
skid as spacers to allow sling lifting of the crate from the ends and forklift handling from the 
sides; 
 
Ensure that all the necessary lifting signs and logistic signs (Fragile handle with care, use no 
hooks, this way up, keep away from heat, sling here, center of gravity, do not roll etc.) are 
made available on the crate; 
 
Ensure that the skids of large crates are heavy enough to allow a notch at each end for the 
sling; and

Wood crates shall be used only for single transportation and it shall not be re-used. 
7.4.14. HELICOPTER LIFTING 
Helicopter flying with under slung loads (USL) requires special precautions to be taken if both the 
helicopter crew and third parties are to be protected from undue risk. Civil Aviation Authority Publication- 
“CAP 426”- Helicopter External Load Operations contains advice on various aspects of external load 
operations for the benefit of operators, pilots and persons supervising the securing and detaching of loads. 
A full and comprehensive briefing and a formal risk assessment shall be carried out with all staff involved 
in the operation. 
 
Reference may also be made to: 
1. CAP 428 – Safety Standards at Unlicensed Aerodromes (including Helicopter Landing Sites); 
2. Health and Safety Executive (HSE) legislation and guidance contained within the Lifting Operations 
and Lifting Equipment Regulations 1998 (LOLER); and 
3. HSE Provision and Use of Work Equipment Regulations 1998 (PUWER). 
HSE information can be accessed from www.hse.gov.uk. 
 
In simple terms the CAA holds the duty of regulation above the point at which the load is released from 
the helicopter and the HSE legislation applies to the maintenance and inspection of all equipment below 
the point of release. 
7.5. 
LIFTING OF PERSONNEL REQUIREMENTS 
7.5.1. INTRODUCTION 
Transporting or moving personnel with the aid of a lifting appliance involve high risk. Personnel lift or 
transfer operations using a lifting appliance and personnel carriers shall only be undertaken when the 
particular circumstances make it essential, and it is not reasonably practicable to lift or transfer personnel 
by less hazardous means. 
Lifting of Personnel shall be avoided unless the risk has been demonstrated as being ALARP. 
Lifting of Personnel is considered Non-routine Complicated and shall be controlled as per applicable 
measures specified in this standard. 
7.5.2. GENERAL REQUIREMENTS

7.6.1. ANOMALY REGISTER 
The Divisions/Contractor shall monitor both operational and equipment integrity through an anomaly

register. Initially any unplanned event shall be highlighted through a Near Miss Report. Depending on 
the event, there may be a need for further examination through ADNOC incident reporting system.

MAINTENANCE & INSPECTION

This section details the maintenance and inspection process applicable to all lifting and hoisting operations 
across ADNOC Group, including contracted work. 
All ADNOC Group owned and Contractor owned lifting equipment maintenance schedule shall be based 
on manufacturer’s recommendations, operating experience, applicable standards and failure modes. 
Maintenance schedule shall integrate preventative and predictive maintenance techniques and shall 
consider the effects of ageing and equipment utilization. 
 
7.7.1. MAINTENANCE AND INSPECTION RESPONSIBILITIES 
The Owner (ADNOC Group/Contactor) shall ensure that every item of lifting equipment is maintained in 
good working order and that periodic maintenance documents are maintained. The scope, methods and 
standards of the operations, maintenance and inspection shall be specified for all types of equipment. 
Maintenance and pre-certification in-house inspection for lifting equipment are the responsibility of the 
concerned Site Maintenance Team prior to deploy Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector to ensure all 
equipment are ready for the inspection & certification. 
7.7.2. MAINTENANCE AND INSPECTION PLANNING  
 
All maintenance and inspection activities performed on lifting equipment either, routine or non-
routine, shall be initiated, planned and tracked according to the guidelines/ requirements of ADNOC 
Group. Certificates, reports and results from maintenance and inspections shall be updated in 
applicable records. It is essential to track all activities and record results such that trends (especially 
degradation of equipment) can be identified, monitored and reviewed. 
Any further works required as a result of inspection and maintenance can be raised through the 
appropriate Work Order (Corrective, Preventative, Breakdown, Shutdown and Service). 
 
Respective Site authorities shall ensure that the Contractor is applying Planned Maintenance, 
inspection and remedial activities for all applicable lifting equipment. TPAs shall be used wherever 
specified in this standard and it shall be qualified by ADNOC and listed in MMS directory. 
 
All Lifting Equipment identified as HSECES, shall have their maintenance strategies and specific 
procedures aligned to the requirements of the applicable HSECES Performance Standard and 
Minimum Assurance Tasks reporting. 
 
7.7.3. MAINTENANCE PROCESS 
 
An effective planned maintenance system shall incorporate means of preventing the use of the 
Lifting equipment until essential maintenance work is carried out. 
A record shall be kept for the Lifting equipment, giving information on the major components used 
in the lifting equipment manufacture, e.g. rope diameters, lengths, construction and breaking loads, 
make and model of motors, pumps, gear boxes, winches, drives, electrical and hydraulic equipment 
and switch gear etc. 
 
Corrective work orders shall be raised for necessary remedial action resulting from inspections, 
technical integrity assessments, Preventive Maintenance Routines (PMR), pre-use tests,

Effective date: August 2019 
Page 54 of 301 
breakdowns, incidents, modifications etc. All corrective tasks shall be carried out to approved 
component repair and overhaul procedures defined in the individual equipment manuals as well as 
the Maintenance Strategy. 
 
The objective of the refurbishment and repair process is to address malfunctions by restoring the 
Lifting Equipment to (or as close as is reasonably practicable) the original design condition using 
the most cost-effective intervention or repair method. 
In some circumstances a temporary repair may be acceptable until a permanent repair can be 
implemented. In such cases it shall be clearly demonstrated, documented and communicated that 
the Lifting Equipment integrity and safety level is being maintained either by the temporary repair 
itself and/or in combination with other precautions, such as reduced Safe Working Load (De-rate 
the Lifting Equipment). The maximum period shall be specified before a permanent repair solution 
shall be implemented. Management of Change process shall be followed for all temporary repairs 
and/or modifications. 
Lifting Accessories (except Lifting Beams) which have been found to be damaged shall be 
quarantined. No repairs shall be attempted on such equipment. 
 
For ADNOC Group owned equipment, adequate spares according to the ADNOC Guidelines for 
Spare Parts Management shall be available for the lifting equipment undergoing the maintenance.   
For contracted equipment, as part of the contract, a minimum level of spares and equipment can be 
assured via the contractors’ own maintenance and spares management systems. 
 
7.7.4. INSPECTION AND TESTING   
 
All statutory lifting equipment inspections and monitoring shall be carried out by a TPA qualified by 
ADNOC. The statutory inspection intervals and requirements detailed in Table 7.7.1 and Appendix 
12 shall be followed.  
The Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector shall carry out the mandatory 6 monthly/ annual 
inspections and functional/performance testing including 48/60 months thorough examination/ proof 
load testing (Refer to Appendix 5) following major maintenance (when applicable) of all fixed and 
portable lifting equipment. When the equipment is deemed to be satisfactory for use, a certificate is 
issued with an expiry date. Portable lifting equipment including CCU’s, loose gears and portable 
lifting appliances under 6-months inspection shall be marked using a color code system as per 
ADNOC Group requirements. The inspection program is aligned with the legislative requirements 
for 6 monthly/12 monthly and 48/60 months inspections.

Thorough Examination  
Lifting Equipment shall undergo detailed/ thorough examination at least every 12 months, and 
at least every 6 months for equipment used to lift people. Thorough examination and 
certification of Lifting Accessories shall not exceed (6) months intervals. Refer to Appendix 
5. 
As a part of thorough examination- visual inspection; functional test; performance test; proof 
load test and/ or Non-Destructive Examination shall be carried out or witnessed by the Third 
Party Inspector as per the intervals set out in Table 7.7.1 and Appendix 12, and provide 
certificates of Thorough Examination, as per the Lifting Equipment Inspection Templates, as 
shown in Appendix 7. 
 
Functional Test 
Functional Test is carried out to check the equipment under inspection to perform movements 
without any load to verify the proper working of safety devices. A working test of all mandatory 
safety devices is to be carried out. 
 
Performance Test 
Performance Test is carried out to check the equipment under inspection to perform 
movements with load to verify all movements including critical safety devices such as 
Overload Alarm or Overload Cut-off. 
 
Proof Load Test 
A Thorough check shall be carried out before and after the Proof Load Test. Periodic Proof 
Load Test for each fixed lifting appliance shall be carried out as per specific Method 
Statement/ Action Plan submitted by TPIA and approved by SPA. Proof load test shall be 
performed when the lifting appliance is new, after installation, after any alteration or major 
repair, after accident and at intervals set out in Appendix 12. Proof load shall be also carried 
out for all newly fabricated lifting accessories and in the absence of an appropriate certificate 
indicating that the item has previously been tested or following any repair or alteration which 
may affect the strength of the item. Scope of overload testing shall include inhibition of 
overload protection system (where fitted) to allow lifting of overload and verification of its 
proper adjustment and functionality after removal of inhibition. Specific statement in the 
certificate shall reflect proper completion of this task. 
 
A thorough examination is to be carried out after test to check that the lifting appliance 
withstood the Overload without damage or permanent deformation. Permanent deformations, 
if any, shall be measured and recorded for further analysis. During the inspection of the 
structures, the competent person shall pay particular attention to deformations due to shocks 
or excessive stresses, cracks, corrosion, inappropriate fittings, etc. The elements that the 
competent person considers necessary to dismantle shall be removed and re-assembled by 
skilled personnel under the responsibility of the owner. 
In the event of any alteration or repair that could affect the integrity of the lifting appliances or 
accessories, testing shall be carried out in accordance with the OEM specification to ensure 
that all parts affected by the repair are subjected to the test loads in accordance with the OEM 
specification. It is necessary to confirm with the manufacturer or other design authority the 
loads to be applied following such alterations or repairs. Management of Change process 
shall be followed for all temporary repairs and/or modifications 
 
Non-Destructive Examination 
On the successful completion of the tests, a thorough examination of the lifting appliances or 
accessories will be undertaken by the Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector to ensure no 
damage or permanent deformation has taken place. The Third Party Lifting Equipment 
Inspector might consider it appropriate to apply NDT techniques to assess the integrity of

## Page 57

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 57 of 301 
components and will advise the maintenance team and/or site authority for the required load 
bearing parts of the lifting equipment that need NDT and the recommended NDT technique. 
 
Non-routine inspections shall be carried out (In house and/or TPA where required) on the Lifting 
Equipment in response to certain conditions, emergencies or events. Examples are: 
 
Failure incident; 
 
Dropped object incident with potential to cause integrity incident; 
 
Confirmed or suspected third party interference incident; 
 
Overload; 
 
Subject to modification or repair to components in the load path or other safety critical 
components; and  
 
Changes in conditions of use including periods of out-of-service for 6 months (Idle and not 
maintained). Refer to Section 4.1.8 of Appendix 5. 
Standard procedures and work instructions depending on the event and the asset(s) affected shall 
be used to carry out such non-routine inspections.   
When preparing work scopes for non-routine inspections, Emergency Response procedures shall 
be complied with where applicable. 
 
 
Inspection period of any lifting equipment shall not be extended, as it is governed by statutory 
requirements, however limited endorsement can be given to a Lifting Equipment following a 
Thorough Examination by TPIA. 
7.7.5. SERVICE LIFE OF LIFTING EQUIPMENT 
Although there is no Industrial Standard that dictates the Lifting Equipment Lifespan; the Manufacturer 
shall provide the Owner the expected lifespan based on duty cycle. If Manufacturer’s data not available, 
following criteria shall be followed: 
 
 
 
7.8. 
COLOR CODING  
The color coding system is add-on for visual identification to indicate that, the lifting accessories/ lifted 
equipment used has undergone its mandatory 6 monthly examination (Twice Yearly). The color code 
changing interval is 6 months. Lifting accessories/ lifted equipment shall only be used if they are displaying 
the current color code. Lifting accessories, which are incorrectly color coded shall be stored in a separate, 
clearly marked area and returned for re-inspection, certification and color coding as per ADNOC 
requirement.
