7.9. 
COMPETENCY 
This section defines the Qualification and Competence requirements of the key personnel engaged in 
lifting and hoisting operation. 
 
7.9.1 
LIFTING TECHNICAL AUTHORITY 
 
Lifting Technical Authority shall:  
 
Hold bachelor’s degree in Mechanical/Electrical Engineering or equivalent; 
 
Hold sufficient Diplomas from the below relevant to the area of operations in addition to the 
Entry Certificate from Lifting Equipment Engineers Association (LEEA, UK) or equivalent: 
1. Lifting Equipment General (Lifting Gear General) 
2. Lifting Machines Manual 
3. Lifting Machines Power 
4. Runways & Crane structure 
5. Overhead Travelling and Portal Cranes 
6. Offshore Containers 
7. Mobile Cranes 
 
Be trained and certified for Appointed Person Course from CPCS A61 Category (Construction 
Plant Competence Scheme) from UK or equivalent which is approved by ADNOC; 
     Pastel Orange RAL 
Grass Green RAL 6010 
Traffic White RAL 9016 
Signal Blue RAL 5005

Have 10 years proven experience of supervising and managing site lifting operations, 
maintenance, inspection & load testing activities for onshore/offshore Lifting Equipment 
including Pedestal Cranes, Gantry Cranes, Runway beams, Mobile Cranes, Crawler Cranes, 
Fork Lifts, Winches, Electric Hoists, Pull Lifts, Chain Blocks, Wire Rope Slings, Webbing 
Slings, Chain Slings, Shackles, Eye Bolts, Lifeboat Davits/Winches, Containers/Baskets. 
Minimum 5 years hands-on-experience on maintenance, inspection, load testing & 
certification, trouble shooting, auditing of cranes and other fixed/loose lifting items; 
 
Have Technical and Management System Audit Skills with TPI Certification Audits; 
 
Be able to conduct incident investigations on lifting related incidents; 
 
Be able to prepare Lifting plans, supervise and execute critical lifting in oil & gas companies; 
 
Be able to manage the process for verifying the competency of Lifting Team (Appointed 
Person, Lifting Supervisor, Crane Operator and Rigger/Banksman), as defined in Appendix 
13; 
 
Have broad based knowledge and conversant with International Standards relevant to Lifting 
Equipment and Operations; 
 
Remain up to date with Health & Safety at Work Guidelines; 
 
Have thorough knowledge of Local Legislations; 
 
Have skills in Contracts Management of Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspection; 
 
Have excellent organizational, problem Solving, contractors handling & communication Skills;  
 
Have proven QA/QC Experience; 
 
Have adequate knowledge in basic Computer Software related applications and reporting; 
 
Have Leadership & Communication skills, High level of Self-Motivation, Organize & Mange 
Team and ability to achieve orientation & mange emergencies; 
 
Be Proficient in English Language; 
 
Have Interpersonal, influencing, facilitation and communication skills; 
 
Be hard working and adopts to work pressures; and 
 
Be a Team player. 
7.9.2 
SINGLE POINT AUTHORITY 
 
 
Hold bachelor’s degree in Mechanical Engineering or equivalent; 
 
Have relevant oil & gas offshore and onshore lifting related experience of not less than 7 
years with minimum 5 years in a similar position; 
 
Have adequate training and hands on professional experience and be competent to act as a 
Single Point Authority for lifting equipment; 
 
Have professional qualification in ISO 9000 like Lead Auditor or similar bodies with sufficient 
experience in Quality Management Systems; 
 
Be able to write Lifting plans, supervise and execute critical lifting in oil & gas companies; 
 
Have focus on HSE priorities and lead a team of engineers for Safe Lifting Practices across 
different locations of the organization; 
 
Be familiar with international standard related to Lifting engineering practices with sufficient 
knowledge in interpretation and to bring excellence in Change management; and

 Be trained to the 4-day course of Appointed Person in addition to the 4-day course of Lifting

7.9.3 
APPOINTED PERSON 
 
Appointed Person shall:  
 
Hold 3 years Diploma in Mechanical Engineering or equivalent;  
 
Have relevant oil & gas offshore and onshore lifting related experience of not less than 7 
years with minimum 5 years in a similar position; 
 
Have adequate training and hands on professional experience and be competent to act as 
an Appointed Person for lifting equipment; 
 
Be able to write Lifting plans, supervise and execute critical lifting in oil & gas companies; 
 
Have focus on HSE priorities and lead a team of Crane Operators, Riggers and Banksmen 
for Safe Lifting Practices across different locations of the organization; 
 
Be familiar with international standard related to Lifting engineering practices with sufficient 
knowledge in interpretation and to bring excellence in Change management; and 
 
Be trained for the 4-day course of Appointed Person by an ADNOC Group approved Training 
Company using approved Tutor and be assessed every 3 years, as defined in Appendix 13. 
He shall be trained and assessed on the same type of offshore or onshore Crane i.e. offshore 
Appointed Person Course is different than Mobile Crane Appointed Person Course. 
7.9.4 
CRANE OPERATOR 
 
Crane Operator shall: 
 
Be minimum 21 years of age; 
 
Be educated to a minimum qualification of higher secondary school certificate or equivalent; 
 
Have at least 5 years of experience as Crane Operator in offshore/onshore, Oil/Gas 
companies with minimum 3 years’ experience in operating similar Crane Type; 
 
Have Valid License to operate the specific equipment from the UAE Traffic Department (in 
case of Mobile Cranes); 
 
Be capable of understanding the English language (Reading, Writing and Speaking) sufficient 
for the fulfilment of their function in a safe manner;  
 
Be physically fit with regard to eyesight, hearing, reflexes and ability to operate crane; 
 
Be familiar with the equipment manufacturer’s instructions for the operation and its basic 
maintenance; 
 Be fluent with the hand signals adopted at the site where the lifting operation taking place; 
 
Be trained for the 3-days course, by an ADNOC Group approved Training Company using 
approved Tutor for specific Crane type and be assessed every 2 years, as defined in 
Appendix 13. Reference shall be made to the below notes for clarity; 
 
Hold certificate of competency issued by ADNOC Group approved Training Company using 
approved Tutor on completion of above stated course; and

LIFTING EQUIPMENT OPERATOR 
 
Forklift Operator shall:

 Be physically fit with regard to eyesight, hearing, reflexes and ability to operate the forklift;

Hold certificate of competency issued by ADNOC Group approved Training Company using

 Be able to operate fire extinguishers equipment; 
 
Be able to resist pressures from other persons to carry out unsafe operations;

7.9.6 
RIGGER/SLINGER 
 
Rigger/Slinger shall: 
 
Be educated to a minimum qualification of secondary school certificate (Grade 10) or 
equivalent; 
 
Be capable of understanding the English language (Reading, Writing and Speaking) sufficient 
for the fulfilment of their function in a safe manner; 
 
Have at least following experience for relevant lifting operations 
1. For Rigger Level-1: 6 months of experience; 
2. For Rigger Level-2: 2 years of experience; and  
3. For Rigger Level-3: 4 years of experience. 
 
Be trained for 2-days course, by an ADNOC Group approved Training Company using 
approved Tutor and be assessed every 2 years, as defined in Appendix 13; 
 
Hold certificate of competency issued by ADNOC Group approved Training Company using 
approved Tutor on completion of above stated course; 
 
Be able to establish weights and the effect of the centre of gravity, to balance loads and judge 
distances, heights and clearances; 
 
Be physically capable of handling lifting equipment and accessories; and 
 Be trained to use man-baskets and conversant with the relevant lifting procedures. 
Rigger Levels fall into three (3) categories; Level 1, Level 2 and Level 3. Each level is allocated a 
permissible lift category as given in Table 7.9.1: Rigger Levels for Lift Categories below. It is 
important to note that a rigger shall perform lift categories only for which he has been assessed.

7.9.8 
LIFTING EQUIPMENT MAINTENANCE PERSONNEL 
 
Lifting Equipment Maintenance Personnel shall: 
 
Be educated to a minimum qualification of higher secondary school certificate or equivalent; 
 
Be capable of understanding the English language (Reading, Writing and Speaking) sufficient 
for the fulfilment of their function in a safe manner; 
 
Have relevant oil & gas offshore and onshore maintenance related experience of not less 
than 5 years in the same area of application; 
 
Have adequate training and hands on professional experience and be able to trouble shoot, 
carry out repairs, overhauling and maintenance of lifting equipment; 
 
Be fully knowledgeable of the equipment’s operating functions and safety systems; 
 
Be familiar with the operational sensitivities and limitations of the equipment; 
 
Be familiar with overhauling and repairs with sufficient knowledge in interpretation with team; 
 Be conversant with procedures and precautions for testing individual drives, braking systems 
and safety systems and controlled overload testing of the equipment; 
 
Be fully conversant with the duties of the operator, and if necessary trained and certified from 
ADNOC approved Training Company to operate the equipment in order to fulfil servicing 
activities; 
 
Be fully conversant with the duties of the operator, and if necessary trained and certified from 
ADNOC approved Training Company to operate the equipment in order to fulfil servicing 
activities; and 
 
Be trained in emergency recovery procedures arising from power failure or severe 
overloading of the equipment. 
7.9.9 
THIRD PARTY LIFTING EQUIPMENT ENGINEERS AND INSPECTORS 
 
Third Party Lifting Equipment Engineer shall:  
 
Hold a bachelor’s degree in Mechanical Engineering or equivalent; 
 
Hold an Entry Certificate issued by the Lifting Equipment Engineers Association (LEEA, UK) 
along with following two Diplomas as minimum: 
 Lifting Equipment General (Lifting Gear General) 
 Lifting Machines Manual

7.10. DOCUMENTATION 
7.10.1. CRITICAL RECORDS 
The critical records shall be stored, maintained and updated as part of the ADNOC Group HSE 
Management System process. Other information deemed necessary for lifecycle integrity management 
shall also be retained in accordance with the Critical Records Policy. 
 
All lifting equipment shall be registered in Official Database prior to use. As a minimum, all lifting equipment 
shall be tagged and where appropriate assigned a unique identifier (Asset Number). The tags shall be 
entered into the Master Equipment List (MEL). The equipment tags shall be structured hierarchically in 
parent child relationships to provide easy maintenance scheduling. To facilitate the capture and retrieval 
process for risk management data each equipment shall have its relevant equipment class assigned along 
with the associated failure class. 
 
All Operations and Maintenance manuals, engineering drawings, system schematics, spare parts dossiers 
and records/ results of maintenance of all lifting equipment shall be kept updated and reflect the as built 
condition of the lifting system and its component parts. As a minimum, one copy of the data and 
information (electronic or paper) should reside both offshore and onshore and the master copies 
maintained. 
 
Records of all lifting equipment periodic thorough examination either owned by ADNOC or Contractors, 
records of all  as well as Records of Training and Competency of lifting equipment personnel, e.g. Lifting 
Equipment Operators, Riggers, Banksman, Lifting Supervisors, Appointed Persons etc. shall be 
maintained and  kept updated as part of the ADNOC Group requirements. 
 
7.10.2. SITE COORDINATION DURING INSPECTION & CERTIFICATION 
Prior to demobilization from the site after performing examination and testing of lifting equipment, the Third 
party inspector shall provide preliminary written report with statement of fitness for satisfactory equipment 
and / or defect report for equipment found unsafe with comments and recommendations; preliminary report 
shall bear inspector’s signature and TPA stamp and shall serve as a legal document until receipt of the 
official certificate/defect report. 
7.10.3. DOCUMENT RETENTION POLICY 
All critical records and documents used for all Lifting related activities shall be managed in compliance 
with the ADNOC Group Document Retention Policy. 
 
COMPLIANCE ASSURANCE 
Regular Internal and External audits of Lifting Equipment Assets and Process for all ADNOC Group 
Locations shall be planned and implemented annually. 
ADNOC Group Companies reserves the right to carry out scheduled or unscheduled external audits, 
reviews and surveillance of the suppliers, sub-contractors and vendors. These audits will be carried out by

ADNOC Group or Third party auditors appointed by ADNOC Group. ADNOC Group will give notice of any

REFERENCES  
 
 
Standard Documents equivalent to those referred to herein shall not be substituted without written 
approval from ADNOC Group. Approval of equivalent Standard Documents will not, in any way, 
remove responsibility from the Contractor, manufacturers or third parties to meet the best practices 
and/ or requirements of the Technical Standard Documents referred to herein, in the event of conflict. 
Where differences and/or conflicting issues occur between the referenced documents themselves 
or the requirements of this Standard, the requirements of this Standard shall overrule unless 
otherwise advised by ADNOC Group Standards Authority/Technical Custodian however, all conflicts 
shall be reported in writing to the ADNOC Group Standards Authority/ Technical Custodian. 
The following order of precedence to standards shall be followed: 
 
Compliance to the Laws of the U.A.E. 
 
Whenever ADNOC Group Technical Standards Documents (TSDs) relevant to the system, 
service and/ or equipment design is available, the same shall be utilized next in the hierarchy 
 
Shareholder Standards Documents shall be utilized next in the hierarchy, if the relevant 
subject is not covered by ADNOC Group standards. 
 
National or International standards (tailored to suit ADNOC Group needs) shall be utilized, if 
the required subject is not covered either by ADNOC Group or Shareholder Standard 
Documents. 
The Contractors/ manufacturers or third parties shall equip themselves with copies of all the 
referenced Standard Engineering Documents referred in Table 9.1 below and shall make them 
readily available to all ADNOC Group or nominated representative/ personnel involved in the work. 
 
Any Changes shall be managed based on Concession Request Forms.

WIRE ROPE AND FITTINGS 
BS EN 12385-1 specifies the general requirements for the manufacture and testing of steel wire ropes. 
Requirements of steel wire ropes for general lifting applications are specified in BS EN 12385-4.  
Other equivalent standard for wire ropes used in Petroleum and Natural gas industries is API Spec. 9A. 
Wire Ropes with Independent Wire Rope Core (IWRC) shall only be used at ADNOC. Wire ropes with 
fiber core (FC) are not permitted. 
The following minimum information should be supplied with an enquiry or order: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
WIRE ROPE SLINGS INCLUDING GROMMETS AND CABLE-LAID SLINGS 
BS EN 13414 specifies the construction requirements, calculation of WLL, verification, certification and 
marking of steel wire rope slings for general lifting service. 
IMCA M 179 gives advice on construction, rating, testing, certification, examination and use of Grommets 
and Cable-laid slings. 
Steel Ferrule secured, Flemish eye Spliced Wire Rope Slings with Independent Wire Rope Core (IWRC) 
shall only be used at ADNOC Locations. Wire Rope Slings with fiber core (FC) and Aluminium Ferrules 
are not permitted for use at offshore locations. The Minimum Accepted Factor of Safety for any Sling 
Type is 4:1. 
The following minimum information should be supplied with an enquiry or order:

HMPE (HIGH MODULUS POLYETHYLENE) ROPES (DYNEEMA) 
HMPE (Dyneema) Ropes shall only be used for Mooring Winches and Inlet Screen Winches designed in 
compliance with BS EN 14492 and shall not be used for General Lifting Slings; however Heavy 
Engineered Lifting Operation Slings are accepted to be used in ADNOC Projects provided that the 
manufacturer and manufacturing process shall be in full compliance to this section. 
 
 SYSTEM APPROACH FOR ROPE & SLING DELIVERY FOR ENGINEERED LIFTS 
The delivery and use of slings, as covered in this paragraph, for engineered lifting operations as defined 
in IMCA SEL 019/IMCA M187. 
 
Safety and reliability of engineered lifting operations depend on lifting equipment and accessories that 
coherently function as intended. Assuring reliability for delivery (BEFORE USE = FIT FOR PURPOSE) 
and in service (AFTER DELIVERY = FIT FOR SERVICE) requires a system approach stipulating 
adequate acceptance criteria for delivery as well as adequate limits to operations as per requirements 
described in paragraphs below. 
 
 SPECIFICATIONS 
HMPE (Dyneema) Ropes shall only be used for Mooring, Inlet Screen Winches and Engineered Onshore 
and Offshore lifts Slings. These ropes shall be designed, manufactured, tested and certified in 
accordance with DNVGL OS-E303 or equivalent with minimum Factor of Safety (7:1) for Inlet Screen 
Winch while minimum Factor of Safety (4:1) for Mooring Winches and Engineered onshore/offshore lifts 
Slings. 
 
The available ISO standards (such as, but not limited to, ISO 14909 & 18264) shall form the basis for 
applicable testing prior to delivery (Strength & Stiffness, linear density, cyclic loading endurance, particle 
ingress resistance, creep property test, core tenacity test, torque properties test …etc.) and shall be 
carried out by the manufacturer and Certification by the Classification Society; however in-service 
Inspection can be carried out by Certification Body also. 
 
The fiber used in the load bearing core of the rope shall be high-tenacity HMPE (Dyneema), with an 
average tenacity of not less than 2,5 N/tex.

PEDESTAL CRANE REQUIREMENTS FOR PERSONNEL LIFTING 
 
 
(c) 
The minimum accepted Standard for Pedestal Crane Design Requirements for Personnel Lifting is 
API Specs. 2C; 6th Edition (2004). API Specs. 2C; 7th Edition did not dictate for the owners to 
modify their Cranes to suit the 7th Edition; however, the following additional points shall be noted 
in addition to the Lifting of Personnel Requirements mentioned in Section 7.5 of this Standard and 
Error! Reference source not found.. 
(i) 
Hoist Overload Alarm in compliance with BS 7262 that activates the Alarm at 90% SWL for 
On-board and Off-board Personnel Mode shall be available. 
(ii) 
Hoist Overload Cut-off in compliance with BS 7262 that activates the Cut-off at 110 % SWL 
for On-board and Off-board Personnel Mode shall be available. 
 
 
 
PEDESTAL CRANE REQUIREMENTS FOR MATERIAL LIFTING  
 
 
 
(i) 
Classification Society Design Approval for the whole Crane including Load Chart 
Endorsement for any Pedestal Crane mounted on-board of Jack-up Barge or Vessel. 
(ii) 
On-board (Static) and Off-board (Dynamic) Load Charts from the Crane’s Manufacturer shall 
be available and shall be endorsed by the Class in case of Jack-up Barges or Vessels 
Pedestal Crane. 
(iii) 
Safe Load Indicator or Rated Capacity Indicator for any Crane with SWL 1 t and above as 
per Abu Dhabi Law (OSHAD) CoP-034 and shall have means of configuration change as 
per the used Load Chart.

## Page 88

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
Pedestal Crane Requirements  
APPENDIX 3 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 88 of 301 
(iv) 
Data Recording Function on the Main/Auxiliary Hoists, as well as a functional Boom Angle 
Indicator; both shall be visible by the Operator for any Pedestal Crane belongs to ADNOC. 
The Data Recording Function is optional for Contractors. 
(v) 
All Cranes with Single Swing-drives and/or Single Brake Drives shall be equipped with a 
Mechanical Anti-rotation Lock. Positive Rotation Locking Devices shall be used: 
1. 
Whenever the Crane Boom is out of the Rest Position and the Crane is unattended 
(Crane shall not be unattended when engine is running). 
2. 
During Helicopter Operations. 
  
(vi) 
Hoist Limit Switch (Rotary Type) in Winch or Chandelier Type (Anti-Two-Block) for 
Main/Auxiliary Hoist Circuits of all Cranes.  
(vii) 
The Hoist Limit Switch System shall be of such type that prevents/ stops the hoist up and 
the boom hoist down functions. 
(viii) The Hoist Limit Switch when activated shall inhibit the Boom down operation (And Extend 
the Boom in case of Telescopic Type) regardless the Hoist Drum is mounted on the Boom 
or on the turret (Structure); this is to prevent damage to crane components and boom 
structure including wire rope; however it shall be noted that some Pedestal Cranes Hoist 
Limit Switch do not inhibit the boom down operation but still prevent damage to crane 
components and boom structure including wire rope despite the Hoist Drum is mounted on 
the turret (Structure); therefore end user and TPI Inspectors shall verify this point on case-
by-case basis during the annual and/or 5 years thorough examinations. 
(ix) 
Boom Upper/Lower Limit Switches to be provided for all Pedestal Cranes with Lattice Booms 
that are raised and lowered by winches; however it is not required for the Hydraulic Rams 
(Cylinders) System Cranes. 
(x) 
Mechanical Boom Angle Indicator (Inclinometer). 
(xi) 
Boom Swing Limit Switch or Motion Sensor in the Crane’s Safe Load Indicator (if collision 
with other Crane or Structure is possible). 
(xii) 
Hoist Overload Alarm in compliance with BS 7262 that activates at 90% SWL and Overload 
Cut-off Limiter at 110% SWL On-board (Static), Off-board (Dynamic) Lifts. 
(xiii) Emergency Stop Switch. 
 
(i) 
Hoist Overload Alarm activates at 90% SWL and Cut-off at 110% SWL for On-board Lift 
Mode shall be available. 
(ii) 
Hoist Overload Alarm activates at 90% SWL to 110% SWL for Off-board Lift Mode shall be 
available and without Cut-off Limiter. 
 
(i) 
Crane Group A: Includes mainly cranes which do not handle cargo and which, with the 
exception of hatch cover cranes and hose cranes, are not always exposed to full safe 
working load. Such cranes are characterized by irregular use and longer rest periods. 
Group A is further subdivided as follows: 
1. 
Crane Group A1 includes cranes for the operation of the ship in the harbor such as:

(ii) 
Crane Group B: Primarily includes cranes used for cargo-handling, and which are not always 
exposed to full safe working load. These cranes are characterized by regular use and longer 
rest periods. 
Group B is further subdivided as follows: 
1. 
Crane Group B1 includes shipboard cranes for cargo-handling in the harbor using 
spreaders or hooks, such as:  
 
Container cranes 
 
General cargo cranes 
2. 
Crane Group B2 includes cranes for cargo-handling at sea using hooks, such as: 
 
General cargo cranes 
3. 
Crane Group B3 includes floating cranes for cargo-handling using hooks, such as: 
 
Floating cargo cranes 
(iii) 
Crane Group C: Primarily includes cranes used for cargo-handling, and which are regularly 
exposed to the full or nearly full safe working load. 
Group C is further subdivided as follows: 
1. 
Crane Group C1 includes shipboard cranes for cargo-handling in the harbor using 
grabs, hooks or special load-handling devices, such as: 
 
Grab cranes 
 
Pallet cranes 
2. 
Crane Group C2 includes ship cranes for cargo-handling at sea using garbs, like: 
 
Grab cranes 
 
Lighter cranes 
3. 
Crane Group C3 includes floating cranes for cargo-handling using grabs, such as: 
 
Grab floating cranes 
 
Lighter floating cranes

(iv) 
In case of any change in crane group or safe working load or radius, in addition to an

(f) 
Marine Vessel’s Cranes can also be classified as per ABS Guide for Certification of Lifting 
Appliances (2018) as follows: 
(i) 
“SC”: signified that the vessel or unit has an installed Shipboard crane designed, 
constructed and tested in accordance with the above mentioned “ABS Guide” used for 
lifting a load while the vessel in the harbor or sheltered area. 
(ii) 
“OC”: signified that the vessel or unit has an installed Offshore crane designed, 
constructed and tested in accordance with the above mentioned “ABS Guide” used for 
lifting a load while the vessel at open sea. 
(iii) 
“HC”: signified that the vessel or unit has an installed Heavy Lift crane designed, 
constructed and tested in accordance with the above mentioned “ABS Guide” used for 
lifting a load while the vessel in the harbor or sheltered area or at open sea in very mild 
environmental condition. 
(iv) 
“SP”: signified that the vessel or unit has an installed Special Purpose crane designed, 
constructed and tested in accordance with the above mentioned “ABS Guide” used for 
lifting a load such as (davit for non-life saving, monorail hoist, engine room overhead crane, 
provision crane…etc.) when the davit is subject to dynamic loads due to motion of the 
vessel or there is relative movement between the vessel/unit. 
Reference shall be made to the latest edition of ABS Guide for Certification of Lifting 
Appliances for details. 
(g) 
Marine Vessel’s Cranes can also be classified as per BV Rules for the Certification of Lifting 
Appliances onboard Ships and Offshore Units (BV NR 526 DT R02E) as follows: 
(i) 
“ALP”: For Lifting appliances used at harbor. 
(ii) 
“ALM”: For Lifting appliances used in offshore conditions. 
Reference shall be made to the latest edition of BV Rules for the Certification of Lifting Appliances 
onboard Ships and Offshore Units for details. 
 
PEDESTAL CRANE REQUIREMENTS FOR SUBSEA LIFTING  
 
It is a lifting operation in which a load is intended to be fully submerged as a body in water. This 
has no relation with Crane’s Hook Position (i.e. even if the Hook Block is not submerged in water 
and the Load is fully submerged); some cranes are suitable for subsea lifting, however not all can 
be put in this category. The crane manufacturer shall be contacted for written confirmation of the 
crane’s suitability for subsea lifting before the crane is used. Any other crane subsea systems shall 
not be used without the express, written approval from Lifting Technical Authority. 
 
Crane intended for lowering and retrieval of objects, through the air/water Interface, water-column 
and the seabed. Subsea lifting is divided into 3 different types of operation phases: 
(i) 
Deployment; 
(ii) 
Recovery; and  
(iii) 
Transfer/relocation. 
There should be at least two separate independent main power feeders for a subsea crane. These 
should follow Main Switch Board (MSB) structure, each from a separate section. Complete blackout 
in the power/control system of the crane, while one section of the MSB is failing should be avoided

(i) 
Deployment 
Pre lift: Last stage before lifting the object. Vessel is typically in its final location, the next 
step being commencement of the operations.  
Lift off: Lift off from deck.  
Overboard: Move the object outboard, over the sea.  
Splash zone: Lower the object through the air-water interface, down to where the effects 
from the waves on the object are insignificant. 
Lowering: Lowering the object through the water column down and close to its landing 
location Move: Moving vessel from one location to another. 
Landing: Landing the object on seabed, other structure etc. 
(ii) 
Recovery 
Pre lift: Last stage before lifting the object. 
Subsea lift off: Lifting from subsea location. 
Move: Moving vessel from one location to another. 
Recovery: Lifting the object from subsea location closer to but below air-water interface. 
Splash zone: Lifting the object through the wave influenced part of water column and into 
the air. 
In-boarding: Moving the object from over the sea to inboard. 
Landing: Landing on deck. 
(iii) 
Relocate 
Pre lift: Last stage before lifting the object. 
Subsea lift off: Lifting from subsea location. 
Move: Moving vessel from one location to new location. 
Transfer: Shift the load from one lifting appliance to another. 
Landing: Landing the object on seabed, other structure etc.  
 
(i) 
The Crane Manufacture’s approval on the crane design for subsea lifting operation.  
(ii) 
Class approval on the crane design for subsea lifting operation. 
(iii) 
ADNOC approved TPI/Engineers certificate regarding the suitability of the crane for subsea 
lifting operation. 
 
Subsea capacity charts shall be provided by the Crane’s Manufacturer, approved by the Class and 
shall be posted in the operator’s cabin. 
It shall include environmental condition, out of plane influences, type of operation and operational 
limitations (Design Dynamic Factor, Heel/Trim Inclination, Off-lead/Side-lead, Maximum Operating 
Wind Speed and Significant Wave Height). 
RCI (Rated Capacity Indicator) shall be programmed for subsea duties and has Mode Selector. 
Load charts for subsea handling shall not be confused with load charts for ship-to-ship handling.

Page 92 of 301 
 
Sub-sea Crane Hook Block shall be designed by the Crane’s Manufacturer for the Subsea Lifting 
Operations as the water can leak into the internal parts of a normal Hook Block and damages it. 
Crane’s Components used for Subsea Lifting shall be designed to prevent sea water ingress. 
 
During subsea lifting operations, all accessible wire rope and associated equipment shall be 
checked visually each day. Particular attention shall be paid to the end termination and the part of 
the wire rope entering the end termination. 
A visual inspection of the wire rope; from the winch drum to the hook commensurate with the depth 
to which the hook will be deployed, shall be carried out at weekly intervals during subsea lifting 
operations. At the same time, because multi-strand rope tends to suffer internal damage, local 
reductions in diameter or lay distortions shall be treated with the utmost suspicion as they are likely 
to indicate serious internal degradation. Any anomalies shall be reported immediately and lifting 
operations shall be stopped. 
When a wire rope is not in service, corrosion can take place; particularly within the wire rope where 
sea water may have been trapped by any external dressing. This means, it is necessary to subject 
the rope to appropriate inspection and tests, before putting it back into service. 
For crane wire ropes not in use, when the period of non-use exceeds one month, the wire rope and 
termination shall be subjected to external visual inspection to identify any corrosion or damage 
over the entire length of wire rope, including the end termination. 
Steel wire ropes for subsea lifting shall be galvanized. End terminations shall be of suitable type to 
avoid galvanic corrosion. 
(i) 
For single fall configurations the rope shall be rotation resistant. 
(ii) 
For multi-fall reeving configurations operational measures e.g. anti-twist systems and type 
of rope shall be evaluated to prevent twisting. 
Subsea operations can require significant rope lengths, further, rope tend to experience uneven 
wear. It is therefore important to monitor the use and wear of the steel wire rope, to assess its 
condition and to assure safe operations. 
Note: There is experience showing that steel wire ropes have been worn out after limited numbers 
of lifts only, due to the amount of load cycles caused by use of active heave compensation during 
operation. 
Crushing of the inner wire rope layers on winch drums shall be considered, with special attention 
to the cross-over zones. It is therefore of vital importance that the condition of the entire wire rope 
length, taking part in a subsea lift, is considered checked before the lift is carried out. 
 
Replacement of wire rope for subsea cranes shall be every 12 months, however the replacement 
may be extended beyond 12 months till 60 months during the Crane’s Major Overhaul in which the 
Wire Rope shall be replaced; subject to the following: 
Successful Thorough Visual Examination results from an ADNOC approved Third Party Lifting 
Equipment Inspector on the full Wire Rope (end to end); and Suitable NDT (i.e. Eddy Current, 
Magnaflux …etc.) on the full Wire Rope; then ADNOC approved Third Party Lifting Equipment 
Inspector may endorse the Crane’s Wire Rope for maximum 12 months subject to successful 
through examination and NDT results. 
Wire Rope Cut back from the used part and witnessed by ADNOC approved Third Party Lifting 
Equipment Inspector shall be carried out if any of the above points (a) and (b) cannot be achieved; 
and wire rope Tensile Destructive Test from both, the used and intact parts samples, witnessed by 
ADNOC approved Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector. ADNOC approved Third Party Lifting

Equipment Inspector to compare the Destructive Test Results (ABL) versus Wire Rope

Full records shall be kept for the crane involved in subsea lifting operations. This crane record shall 
be part of the records of the crane’s planned maintenance program, the records shall contain 
details of all daily, weekly inspections and maintenance carried out on the crane. 
 
Full records shall be kept for each rope involved in subsea lifting operations, from its first installation 
to discard. The records shall contain the following information: 
(i) 
At installation, details of the installed wire rope shall include: 
1. 
Date, length installed and name of wire rope manufacturer; 
2. 
Minimum breaking load (MBL) and “Actual Breaking Load” (ABL); and  
3. 
Diameter, construction, wire grade and surface treatment. 
(ii) 
Confirmation of daily and weekly inspections; 
(iii) 
Records of lubrications (date, lubricant used and application method); and  
(iv) 
Record of inspections, maintenance and testing, needs to include: 
1. 
Six months inspection results, 
2. 
Details of any length removed; 
3. 
Details of “NDT”, if applicable; 
4. 
Details of pressure lubrication, if applicable; 
5. 
Details of re-termination; and  
6. 
Results of the functional tests. 
 
 
Mode Selector: 
Subsea lifting appliances shall be equipped with mode selector enabling/disabling safety 
systems according to positioning of the hook. 
 
Mode selector shall be arranged in a way that MOPS and AOPS shall be automatically 
available only for relevant phases of the operation. Where MOPS and AOPS are not 
recommended, those functions shall be automatically blocked. 
 
AHC: 
Active Heave Compensation is a system, which maintains the position of the Wire Rope and 
the load being lifted by a Crane using external energy, also known as Heave or Wave 
Compensation System (This system is optional). 
 
PHC: 
Passive Heave Compensation system is a system, which maintains the position of the load 
by reducing fluctuations in position using stored energy that responds to external actions 
(This system is mandatory for Subsea Cranes). 
 
CT: 
Constant Tensioning is a system aims to achieve a constant tension in the crane’s wire in 
order to keep it vertical using external energy (This system is a shall for Subsea Cranes; 
however it shall not be used in combination with a separate PHC system due to risk of 
interference unless calculations demonstrate that the systems can be combined).

The following Critical Safety Devices shall be available in any Pedestal Crane as follows: 
 
Safe Load Indicator or Rated Capacity Indicator. 
 
Hoist Limit Switch (Anti-Two-Block). 
 
Boom Upper Limit Switch. 
 
Boom Lower Limit Switch. 
 
Boom Swing Limit Switch or Motion Sensor (if collision with other Crane or Structure is 
possible). 
 
Hoist Overload Alarm starts at 90% SWL and Overload Cut-off Limiter at 110% SWL On-
board (Static), Off-board (Dynamic) Lifts and Personnel lifting operation (If Applicable). 
 
Emergency Stop Switch. 
 
MOPS: 
Manual Overload Protection System is a system activated by the Crane Operator that 
protects the crane against possible overload by reducing the load carrying capacity and 
allowing the Hook to be pulled away from the crane in any direction (This system shall be 
de-activated if the Crane is to be used for Personnel Lifting). 
 
AOPS: 
Automatic Overload Protection System is a system that automatically safeguards and 
protects a Crane against the effects of an overload during Lifting Operation’s by allowing 
the Hook to be pulled away from the Crane in any direction without causing significant 
damage to the Crane (This system is optional for Subsea Cranes; however if available shall 
be de-activated if the Crane is to be used for Personnel Lifting).

INTRODUCTION 
This Appendix is intended to provide detailed information for conducting onshore and offshore lifting 
equipment thorough examination. This procedure and practice is provided as guidelines to ADNOC 
Group, Contractors and its subcontractors and shall be implemented and enforced on all ADNOC Group 
locations to achieve safe lifting operations.  
 
Thorough Examination is an assessment carried out by a competent person in such depth and details 
as the competent person (Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector) considers necessary to enable them 
to determine whether the equipment being examined is safe to continue in use. Thorough examination 
includes visual inspection, dimensional measurement, testing (e.g. functional testing, performance 
testing, proof load testing) and non-destructive testing to detect defects or weaknesses in order to assess 
their importance in relation to the safety and continued use of the lifting equipment. 
 
 
GENERAL

ANNUAL THOROUGH EXAMINATION REQUIREMENTS 
All cranes shall be load tested with 100% SWL of Maximum Crane Capacity on annual basis and “6-
monthly for Personnel Lifting”; however Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector during 6-monthly 
thorough examination with Load Test for Personnel Lifting Crane shall issue One Certificate with 
reference to the Valid Annual 100% SWL Load Test Certificate. 
Note-1: It is not allowed to have 2 Valid Certificates for the same Crane i.e. (One for Materials Lifting 
and another for Personnel Lifting) as this is considered illegal. 
Note-2: Any Lifting Equipment shall be thoroughly examined and certified by ADNOC approved Third 
Party Company using approved Lifting Equipment Inspectors following wire rope replacement to ensure 
the proper selection and installation as per OEM, however load test in this occasion is not required. 
Note-3: In case if the crane is having uniform SWL at all radius, the crane shall be tested at 100% SWL. 
Note-4: ADNOC recognized the difficulties of performing Annual 100% SWL Load Test for high Capacity 
cranes; therefore the following Table for minimum Annual Load Test percentage from the Maximum 
Crane Capacity can be adopted; however the actual applied Test Load shall be sufficient to convince the 
Lifting Equipment Inspector that Overload Alarm and Cut-off System are available and functioning as

THOROUGH EXAMINATION OF LIFTING APPLIANCES 
 
PEDESTAL CRANES 
 
Typical Pedestal Crane 
 
PEDESTAL CRANES CLASSIFICATION ACCORDING TO USAGE/DUTY CYCLE 
The Crane usage categories will be assigned by the users on a consistent Crane by Crane basis. 
The intent is to measure their duty cycle as the duration of time for which the crane is in actual use. 
Crane Inspection and Preventive Maintenance developed by the owner can be in accordance with 
Usage/ Duty Cycle and Crane Manufacturer’s Recommendations. 
There are three Crane Usage Categories and as follows: 
 
Infrequent Usage applies to those cranes that are used for 10 hours or less per month, based on 
the averaged use over a quarter. These cranes will be subject to a Pre-use Inspection and an 
Annual Inspection. Crane usage categories will be reviewed on a periodic basis by the Owner to 
ensure proper inspection intervals. 
 
Moderate Usage applies to those cranes that are used for more than 10 hours but for less than 50 
hours per month, based on the averaged use over a quarter. These cranes will be subject to Pre-
use, Quarterly and Annual Inspection. Crane Usage Categories will be reviewed on a periodic basis 
by the Owner to ensure proper inspection intervals. 
 
Heavy Usage applies to those cranes that are used for 50 hours or more per month. These cranes 
will be subject to Pre-use, Monthly, Quarterly and Annual Inspection. Cranes assigned this 
category usage need not be reviewed to determine the number of hours used each month. 
 
 
CRANE USAGE CATEGORIES 
In order to categorize a crane in the Infrequent or Moderate Usage Category, a certain amount of usage 
documentation is required; however the Owner has the option of not documenting crane usage, in which 
case the crane will default to Heavy Usage Category. 
 
 
DETERMINING CRANE USAGE 
The duty cycle or usage on a crane can be determined by keeping a log of actual crane use. The type of 
log used is at the discretion of the Owner. Keeping track of engine run time could also be used, but this

would overestimate the actual crane use time. Other types of usage recording devices, such as drum 
counters, hour meters etc. could be used, and it will be the crane owner’s responsibility to keep track and 
document the duty cycle of each crane. 
 
 
INSPECTION CLASSIFICATION 
 
Prior to first time use, new cranes shall be inspected by a qualified engineer to levels of compliance 
required by the crane manufacturer and to the relevant standard. For certification requirements 
refer to TPIA. 
 
For cranes in regular service, the inspection intervals are dependent upon the nature of the cranes 
components or systems and of the degree of their exposure to wear, deterioration or malfunction 
and of the consequences to operational safety. 
 
Two inspection categories have been assigned herein e.g. 'frequent' and 'periodic'. 
 
Frequent inspection: Visual daily to monthly intervals. 
 
Periodic inspection: At stated intervals which shall include daily and monthly inspection. 
 
ANNUAL INSPECTION 
Each crane shall be inspected fully every 12 months by a qualified ADNOC approved Lifting Equipment 
Inspector in an approved Third Party Company, whereby all remedial work will be identified and cleared 
prior to issue of an inspection certificate. Annual inspection shall include regular inspection schedules. 
 
 
DEFICIENCIES ARISING FROM INSPECTION 
All deficiencies arising from inspections shall be carefully examined for impairing safe operation. In such 
instances the crane shall be taken out of service or its operation limited to remove the unsafe condition; 
however such specific deficiencies/instances shall be discussed between TPI, Integrity, Site and Crane 
Engineer on a case by case basis before any crane is removed form service or certification with-held. 
'CAUTION' notices shall be positioned at the control cabin and at the prime mover as appropriate. 
Where cranes have been subjected to major repair or modification to either structural members or to the 
main functional services, the cranes shall be thoroughly examined including Visual Inspection, 
Functional/Performance Testing and Proof Load Test by an approved Third Party Lifting Equipment 
Inspector. 
 
INSPECTION RECORDS 
Inspection details which include reports as well as records of repairs and modifications carried out on the 
cranes shall be dated and signed for monthly, quarterly and annual inspection. These records shall be 
kept readily available by the operator. 
 
 
CRANES NOT IN REGULAR USE 
Cranes which have been idle for a period of 1 month up to 6 months shall be inspected according to the 
periodic requirements of Regular Inspection, as stated above in Section 4.1.4 (b). 
Cranes which have been idle for a period more than 6 months shall be given a complete inspection 
according to the requirements of Annual Inspection, as stated above in Section 4.1.5. 
 
 
PERIODICITY:  
Refer to inspection legislation matrix in Appendix 12.

  SWING CIRCLE ASSEMBLY 
All slew ring bolts shall be checked for slackness, damage or breaks. Slewing rack and pinion shall be 
checked for cracks, wear and lubrication. 
Failure of slewing rings is a high potential risk with disastrous consequences. In the majority of cases the 
parts prone to failure are totally enclosed and cannot be easily examined at the twelve monthly 
inspections. 
There are four methods to measure the bearing wear condition that shall be monitored to determine its 
expected service life, and the four methods are as follows: 
 
In order to perform this method, it shall be possible to fully tilt the bearing forward and rearward 
using the counterweight of the crane, jacks, boom luffing cylinder, etc. 
 
The objective is to measure the total internal bearing raceway to rolling element clearance with no 
substantial moment on the bearing which could cause elastic deformation. 
 
It is strongly recommended the tilt procedure be performed at a minimum of four locations every 
90° around the circumference of the bearing. 
 
This procedure involves positioning a dial indicator between the rotating and nonrotating bearing 
races. The crane boom is positioned to fully tilt the bearing forward using a light hook load if 
necessary. The dial indicator is positioned under the boom or under the rear of the crane in line 
with the boom centre line and zeroed. The boom is then raised to its highest position to fully tilt the 
bearing rearward. The bearing clearance is then measured on the dial indicator. The bearing 
should again be tilted forward to its original position to verify the accuracy of the measurement by 
the dial indicator again reading zero. 
 
Note: This tilt procedure can only be used if the crane has sufficient counterweight to fully tilt 
rearward when the boom is raised to its highest position. If the crane/bearing is not tilted fully, 
highly inaccurate and misleading clearance measurements will result. For cranes with insufficient 
counterweight, jacks can be used to fully tilt the bearing rearward or on box boom cranes, the 
luffing cylinder can be used to lightly power down against an adequate support to tilt the bearing 
rearward. 
 
 
This system involves the monitoring of bearing wear by periodically measuring the distance 
between two machined surfaces on the front or rear of the crane with the bearing fully tilted forward 
with no excessive moment load. In order for the procedure to be accurate, the measurements shall 
be taken between the exact same surfaces at the exact same point each time with the boom at the 
same position with the same moment load. 
 
The first measurement shall be performed when the bearing is new, and the crane is first put into 
service to obtain a base value for subsequent repeat measurement comparisons.

## Page 105

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
Lifting Equipment Thorough Examination 
 
APPENDIX 5 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 105 of 301 
 
Without having this base value, the depression measurement method cannot be used to determine 
the total wear in the bearing as the bearing is never tilted rearward. 
 
 
When the Tilt Method cannot be used due to insufficient crane counterweight and when no base 
value reading was ever taken for use with the Depression Measurement method, a third method 
shall be used. One possible third method is the Rotation Method. 
The rotation method is based on the fact that a bearing can always be fully tilted forward in the 
direction of the boom and the tilt will follow the rotation of the crane. A dial indicator, with a magnetic 
base, can be fixed to the crane or pedestal with the needle of the indicator positioned on a clean, 
rust free, machined horizontal surface of the bearing or crane. The dial indicator is positioned in 
the front or rear of the crane in line with the boom. The boom shall be positioned such that the 
bearing is fully tilted forward with no excessive moment load. After the indicator is zeroed, the 
crane is slowly rotated 360° with the dial indicator reading recorded every 45° of crane rotation. 
The dial indicator should return to zero when the crane is rotated 360° back to its original position. 
The dial indicator shall be repositioned every 90° in order to perform four (4) individual tests. This 
method may not be as precise as the tilt and depression measurement methods as the machined 
surface of the bearing opposite where it contacts the crane and pedestal flange may not be 
machined perfectly true to the rolling element raceway diameter. However, the rotation method 
does produce reasonably accurate results when performed on a periodic, consistent basis and 
may be the only method that can be used. 
 
 
Wear can be monitored by periodic grease sample analysis and/or a physical inspection of the 
grease. Grease samples shall be collected every twelve (12) months as a minimum; and this period 
should be shortened if obvious metal or contaminants are present. 
At the twelve monthly examinations the bearing should be inspected as far as practicable and the 
behavior of the bearing whilst the crane is in operation observed for any indications of excessive 
running clearance. 
 
It is good practice to measure the slew ring bearing clearance on newly acquired cranes before 
first use to provide a reference value against which all subsequent measurements can be assessed. 
 
 FUNCTIONAL TEST 
Functional testing shall be carried out without a load applied. The object of functional testing is to 
determine whether the equipment performs as the manufacturer intended. This shall include the 
operation of all controls (including any remote controls) to determine whether the equipment operates 
correctly and smoothly and is free from wear and other damage. Functional testing shall be carried out 
on all functions of the crane to check all motions alarms, limit switches, safety devices and brakes where 
it might be necessary to include the lifting of a suitable load once these devices have been tested without 
a load applied. No less than five (5) full wraps of rope shall remain on the drum(s) in any operating 
condition. The drum end of the rope shall be anchored to the drum by an arrangement provided by the 
crane manufacturer. 
 
 PERFORMANCE TEST 
Performance testing shall be carried out after functional testing and with the rated load applied. The 
object of performance testing is to determine whether the equipment performs to the manufacturer’s 
specification. This should include the operation of all controls (including any remote controls) to 
determine whether the equipment operates correctly and smoothly at the rated speeds and is free from 
wear and other damage. Performance testing shall be carried out on all functions of the crane including 
brakes and safety devices.

APPENDIX 5

Effective date: August 2019 
Page 106 of 301 
 LOAD TEST 
Before and after any load testing the equipment shall be thoroughly examined. The objective of load 
testing is to determine whether the equipment is stable, structurally sound and fit for the use for which it 
was designed. The test program drawn up by the competent person should include every load-bearing 
part. The competent person should determine whether the original or if appropriate the previous test 
certificate is satisfactory, and subsequent testing should be based on this with any necessary 
modifications to suit the requirements of the particular item of equipment. To prevent undue repetition, 
the test program should be conducted so that each load-bearing part is given one overload, for example 
to test a wire rope hoist mechanism and brakes it is only necessary to subject the mechanism to the 
overload on maximum line pull 
 
Note: The competent person might wish to consult the manufacturer or other design authority to ensure 
that the selected test program is adequate. 
 
During testing with overloads all operations shall be carried out with extreme care and every permissible 
crane motion carried out singly at the lowest possible speed. Overloads should be kept as close to the 
Platform/Water as possible, generally between 100 mm and 200 mm. It is preferred that overloads are 
not raised above 200 mm to allow them to pass over obstructions. Where such limitations exist the 
competent person should consider an alternative test to prove the crane in the restricted area. When no 
alternative test is possible and the competent person is not satisfied that all of the crane duties have 
been adequately tested, use of the crane should be restricted to the tested duties and both, the certificate 
of test and report of thorough examination endorsed accordingly.  To ensure that the load is applied at 
the correct position, any dimensions shall be checked using separate measuring equipment; the crane 
instrumentation shall not be used for this purpose. All measuring equipment should be accurate to ±1 % 
of the measured value unless specified otherwise and be undamaged and properly maintained. The 
weight of the lifting accessories shall be included as part of the test load. During the test the performance 
of the crane should be monitored by the competent person. 
 
 CRANES AND OTHER EQUIPMENT WHICH HAVE BEEN ALTERED OR REPAIRED 
In the event of any alteration or repair that could affect the stability of the crane, stability testing shall be 
carried out in accordance with the original crane specification. In the event of any alteration or repair 
which affects the strength of the crane, proof load testing shall be carried out to ensure that all parts 
affected by the repair are subjected to the test loads in accordance with the original crane specification. 
 
Note: It might be necessary to confirm with the manufacturer or other design authority the loads to be 
applied following such alterations or repairs. 
 
 PROOF LOAD TEST CRITERIA 
Refer to Inspection Legislation Matrix in Appendix 12. 
 
Third Party Inspection Company to advise the required Method Statement for the proof load test and to 
be sent to ADNOC Asset Integrity Lifting Engineer for review and approval prior to mobilize the Third 
Party Lifting Equipment Inspector to the site. Scope of proof load testing shall include inhibition of 
overload protection system (where fitted) to allow lifting of overload and verification of its proper 
adjustment and functionality after removal of inhibition. 
 
 NON-DESTRUCTIVE TESTING (NDT) TECHNIQUES 
On completion of the tests, a thorough examination of the crane will be undertaken by the competent 
person to ensure no damage or permanent deformation has taken place, the competent person shall 
request to apply appropriate NDT techniques to assess the integrity of components. These techniques 
can assist in the detection of any material cracks or defects that might grow in service and ultimately lead 
to failure. Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector will advise for the required load bearing parts of the 
lifting equipment that need NDT and the recommended NDT technique.

 WIRE ROPES 
Wear occurs on the outer strands of wire ropes where they pass over the various sheaves in their 
respective systems. Sheaves are machined accurately by the crane manufacturers for the diameters of 
wire rope fitted to each crane when new. A check shall be made that any replacement wire ropes are of 
correct diameter and type of construction. Wire ropes fitted to the main and auxiliary hoists are usually 
of a construction intended to reduce twisting of the ropes when lowered through tens of meters and are 
referred to as “non-rotating type”. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Three (3) broken wires within one (1) lay length; and  
 
Two (2) broken wires at the end connection.

 WIRE ROPE REPLACEMENT  
Wire rope will be selected by using the following criteria: 
 
(b) 
Pendants or standing ropes replaced with rope of the same diameter, length, construction and 
grade as originally furnished or as recommended by the crane or rope manufacturer. End 
connections will be the same as originally furnished or as recommended by a crane or rope 
manufacturer; 
(c) 
Load hoist ropes replaced with rope of the same diameter, construction and grade as originally 
furnished or as recommended by the crane or rope manufacturer; and 
(d) 
The end fastening of wire ropes shall be closely examined as per procedure for wire rope fittings 
where these are secured by rope grips, these shall be correctly fitted and there shall be the 
adequate number.

In addition to that all hooks, blocks, etc. shall be inspected by NDT in accordance with the relevant 
procedure supplemented at the discretion of the Inspector. The distance between reference marks made 
by centre punch either side of the hook opening should be measured and recorded on the report of 
thorough examination. 
Reference shall be made to Section 5 of this Appendix for Wire Rope Replacement Interval. 
 
 SWING CIRCLE ASSEMBLY 
All slew ring bolts shall be checked for slackness, damage or breaks. 
Slewing rack and pinion shall be checked for cracks, wear and lubrication. 
Failure of slewing rings is a high potential risk with disastrous consequences. In the majority of cases the 
parts prone to failure are totally enclosed and cannot be easily examined at the twelve-monthly 
inspections. There are four methods to measure the bearing wear condition that shall be monitored to 
determine its expected service life, (Refer to Section 4.1.23; Points (a), (b), (c) and (d) of this Appendix). 
 
 SLEW RING BEARING CLEARANCE MEASUREMENT AND CONDITION ASSESSMENT 
Slew ring bearing clearance measurements shall be made in accordance with the manufacturer’s 
instructions. These are most effectively made in a workshop. In addition to measurement in support of 
thorough examination, the frequency of measurement shall be in line with the manufacturer’s 
recommendations and at an increased frequency if the crane has been subjected to arduous service 
conditions or at the request of the competent person completing thorough examination. The condition 
and function of greasing systems, grease lines, nipples and lip seals should be ascertained. 
 
An indication of slew ring bearing clearance may also be obtained on a jib crane without disassembly. In 
this case measurements should be taken using a dial test indicator (DTI) mounted on the centerline of 
the bearing track. The DTI should be zeroed with no load on the hook (back moment condition). A load 
should then be lifted at such a radius that a forward moment condition is created and the bearing moves 
to the other extent of its play. The process should then be repeated with the crane slewed through 45° 
increments. 
 
The slew ring should also be checked dynamically and noted for noise and smooth operation, condition 
of teeth and pinion gear (mesh and backlash). Measurement of slew ring bearing play on an assembled 
crane will not give a result that is as accurate as axial loading in workshop conditions but can be expected 
to give a good indication of excessive wear and the need for any corrective action. 
 
It is good practice to measure the slew ring bearing clearance on newly acquired cranes before first use 
to provide a reference value against which all subsequent measurements can be assessed. 
 
 FUNCTIONAL TEST 
Functional testing shall be carried out without a load applied. The object of functional testing is to 
determine whether the equipment performs as the manufacturer intended. This shall include the 
operation of all controls (including any remote controls) to determine whether the equipment operates 
correctly and smoothly; and is free from wear and other damage. Functional testing should be carried 
out on all functions of the crane to check all motions alarms, limit switches, safety devices and brakes 
where it might be necessary to include the lifting of a suitable load once these devices have been tested 
without a load applied. No less than three (3) full wraps of rope shall remain on the drum(s) in any 
operating condition. The drum end of the rope shall be anchored to the drum by an arrangement provided 
by the crane manufacturer. 
 
 PERFORMANCE TEST 
Performance testing shall be carried out after functional testing and with the rated load applied. The 
object of performance testing is to determine whether the equipment performs to the manufacturer’s 
specification. This should include the operation of all controls (including any remote controls) to 
determine whether the equipment operates correctly and smoothly at the rated speeds; and is free from

 LOAD TEST 
Before and after any load testing the equipment shall be thoroughly examined. The objective of load 
testing is to determine whether the equipment is stable, structurally sound and fit for the use for which it 
was designed. The test program drawn up by the competent person should include every load-bearing 
part. The competent person should determine whether the original or if appropriate the previous test 
certificate is satisfactory, and subsequent testing should be based on this with any necessary 
modifications to suit the requirements of the particular item of equipment. To prevent undue repetition, 
the test program should be conducted so that each load-bearing part is given one overload, for example 
to test a wire rope hoist mechanism and brakes it is only necessary to subject the mechanism to the 
overload on maximum line pull. 
 
Note: The competent person might wish to consult the manufacturer or other design authority to ensure 
that the selected test program is adequate. 
 
During testing with overloads all operations shall be carried out with extreme care and every permissible 
crane motion carried out singly at the lowest possible speed. Overloads shall be kept as close to the 
ground as possible, generally between 100 mm and 200 mm. It is preferred that overloads are not raised 
above 200 mm to allow them to pass over obstructions. Where such limitations exist, the competent 
person should consider an alternative test to prove the crane in the restricted area. When no alternative 
test is possible and the competent person is not satisfied that all of the crane duties have been adequately 
tested, use of the crane should be restricted to the tested duties and both, the certificate of test and report 
of thorough examination endorsed accordingly.  To ensure that the load is applied at the correct position, 
any dimensions shall be checked using separate measuring equipment; the crane instrumentation shall 
not be used for this purpose. All measuring equipment should be accurate to ±1 % of the measured value 
unless specified otherwise and be undamaged and properly maintained. The weight of the lifting 
accessories shall be included as part of the test load. During the test the performance of the crane should 
be monitored by the competent person. Scope of overload testing shall include inhibition of overload 
protection system (where fitted) to allow lifting of overload and verification of its proper adjustment and 
functionality after removal of inhibition. Specific statement in the certificate shall reflect proper completion 
of this task. 
 
 CRANES AND OTHER EQUIPMENT WHICH HAVE BEEN ALTERED OR REPAIRED 
In the event of any alteration or repair that could affect the stability of the crane, stability testing shall be 
carried out in accordance with the original crane specification. In the event of any alteration or repair 
which affects the strength of the crane, proof load testing shall be carried out to ensure that all parts 
affected by the repair are subjected to the test loads in accordance with the original crane specification. 
Note: It might be necessary to confirm with the manufacturer or other design authority the loads to be 
applied following such alterations or repairs. 
 
 PROOF LOAD TEST CRITERIA:  
Refer to inspection legislation matrix in APPENDIX 12. 
Third Party Inspection Company to advice the required Action Plan for the proof load test and to be sent 
to ADNOC Group for review and approval prior to mobilize the Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector 
to the site. Scope of proof load testing shall include inhibition of overload protection system (where fitted) 
to allow lifting of overload and verification of its proper adjustment and functionality after removal of 
inhibition. 
 
 NON-DESTRUCTIVE TESTING (NDT) TECHNIQUES 
On completion of the tests, a thorough examination of the crane will be undertaken by the competent 
person to ensure no damage or permanent deformation has taken place, the competent person might 
consider it appropriate to apply NDT techniques to assess the integrity of components. These techniques

determine whether the equipment operates correctly and smoothly at the rated speeds; and is free from

rated load at the center of the bridge shall not exceed 1/750 of the span, for cantilever section of the 
beam the maximum deflection with the rated load shall not exceed 1/250 of the span

TOWER CRANES 
A conventional Tower crane is a slewing jib type crane with jib located at the top of a vertical tower which 
is assembled on site from components 
Typical Tower Crane 
  
 
PERIODICITY: 
Refer to Legislation Matrix in Appendix 12. 
 
 
GENERAL 
 
 
 
 
 
 
THOROUGH EXAMINATION 
(a) 
Tower cranes shall be thoroughly examined on the ground before first erection. This not only 
ensures that the crane is in a suitable condition for erection but also enables parts that are not 
easily accessible on an erected crane to be examined;  
(b) 
Prior to thorough examination the base of the crane should be cleaned by appropriate means, 
e.g. pressure washed, to remove all spoil/dirt that would otherwise conceal the structure or 
mechanisms and prevent an effective examination. The examination should be carried out in a 
logical sequence, for example top to bottom, to ensure that nothing is overlooked;  
(c) 
Ensure that the crane is marked with a unique Identification Number or Asset Number, SWL and 
have valid certificate of inspection from an approved Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector

## Page 119

HSE Management System 
Operations Safety Standards 
Lifting and Hoisting Operations 
Lifting Equipment Thorough Examination 
 
APPENDIX 5 
Standard No.: HSE-OS-ST19 
Version No.: 1 
Effective date: August 2019 
Page 119 of 301 
working in approved TPA in UAE. Check the inspection and other maintenance documentation 
is in order and in compliance with the relevant MMS and OEM. This should cover all wire rope, 
blocks and any other ancillary equipment;  
(d) 
Ensure identification and markings are clearly marked (SWL at various span/radii, specific safety 
instructions e.g. “Do not stay under the suspended load” and signals for controlling crane 
operations”;  
(e) 
Thorough examinations of tower cranes shall include confirmation that the RCI/SLI has been 
calibrated to an accuracy within the tolerances given below within the last 12 months. During the 
calibration check of the RCI/L it is essential that the tower crane is not loaded beyond 110 % of 
its rated capacity. The radius/angle at which the test load corresponds to 110 % of the rated 
capacity shall be marked and the test load shall not be taken beyond that point;  
(f) 
Visually examine the crane tower structure, including the cast-in anchors/ cruciform base/ 
chassis/internal climbing collar/grillage, for signs of damage, distortion, cracking and corrosion;  
(g) 
If applicable, visually examine the crane travelling base rail track for signs of damage, distortion, 
cracking and corrosion;  
(h) 
Visually inspect any central (base) ballast of the crane for corrosion, damage, cracks and security. 
Visually check the total mass of the central (base) ballast and positioning of individual weights;  
(i) 
Visually inspect all bolts, pins and other fastenings to check that they are not loose;  
(j) 
Visually examine the slewing mechanism for security and wear during operation of the slew 
motion throughout its full range of movement, and with the tower crane at maximum reach;  
(k) 
Visually examine all electrical cables on the crane for damage, security and fretting;  
(l) 
Visually examine all electrical enclosures on the crane for corrosion, damage and security;  
(m) 
Visually examine all pipework on the crane for corrosion, damage, leakage, security and fretting;  
(n) 
Examine all hydraulic cylinders for leakage, corrosion on the rods and alignment. Visually check 
end fixings for wear, security and lubrication;  
(o) 
Visually inspect the counter jib, slewing platform, A frame, trolley and jib of the crane for corrosion, 
damage, cracks and distortion;  
(p) 
Visually inspect any trolley rope breaks safety device, if fitted, for corrosion, damage, cracks and 
distortion. Check any bearings and pivots for freedom of action;  
(q) 
Visually inspect the counter ballast of the crane for corrosion, damage, cracks and security. 
Visually check the total mass of the counterweight and correct position of individual weights;  
(r) 
Visually examine all pivoting joints on the jib and attachments of the crane for wear, corrosion, 
security and evidence of lubrication;  
(s) 
Visually check whether all pulleys/sheaves and drums are free from damage and wear, whether 
the rope fits correctly on them and if they are effectively lubricated. Check whether all idler 
pulleys/sheaves turn freely and all guards are undamaged and in place;  
(t) 
Visually inspect all motors, drive couplings, gearboxes and winch drums for wear, damage, 
security and leakage;  
(u) 
Visually inspect the braking mechanisms for wear, damage and adjustment and check whether 
it conforms to the instructions for the crane;  
(v) 
Check any means of access for completeness and security of ladders, hoops, walkways, rest 
platforms; handrails/hand holds and safety line anchorages. Examine powered access including 
elevating control stations and access hoists in accordance with the instructions;  
(w) 
Check the oil and other fluid for condition (for example by debris monitoring) and level of the fluid;  
(x) 
Visually inspect the operator’s cab of the crane for corrosion, damage, cracks and security;  
(y) 
Visually check whether the fixings for the operator’s seat are in place and secure;

(z) 
Visually check whether the cab windows are secure (including guard bars) and clean, and

(aa) 
Check whether all controls (including those located in remote control stations where fitted) are

(bb)

(ee)

(ff)

example rating plate for load lifting. 
Note: Tower cranes used to lift persons shall either be examined by a competent person at least once 
every 6 months or at a lesser interval if stipulated by the competent person. Any passenger carrying 
device such as an elevating control station, access hoist or climbing frame shall either be examined by 
a competent person at least once every 6 months or at a lesser interval if stipulated by the competent 
person.  
Climbing frames, whilst not always part of the crane’s permanent equipment, are devices for lifting 
persons and shall be thoroughly examined at intervals not exceeding 6 months. Further advice regarding 
the minimum inspection criteria for examination of climbing frames is given in Annex G of BS 7121-2003. 
The 6-month thorough examination should only be attempted if the top sections of the climbing frame 
have landings that are completely decked out.  
Any repairs/ Alterations on the crane structure shall be done as per approved OEM procedures or in 
consultation with qualified structural engineer. 
 
 
FUNCTIONAL TEST 
Functional testing shall be carried out without a load applied. Operate the tower crane and check whether 
all motions operate smoothly and effectively without excessive play. The load lifting attachment should 
not drop excessively after the motion has been stopped and all limiters and safety devices should operate 
correctly.  
  
 
PERFORMANCE TEST 
Performance testing shall be carried out after functional testing and with the rated load applied. The 
object of performance testing is to determine whether the equipment performs to the manufacturer’s 
specification. This should include the operation of all controls (including any remote controls) to 
determine whether the equipment operates correctly and smoothly at the rated speeds and is free from 
wear and other damage. Performance testing shall be carried out on all functions of the crane including 
brakes and safety devices.  
 
 
LOAD TEST 
Before and after any load testing the equipment shall be thoroughly examined. The objective of load 
testing is to determine whether the equipment is stable, structurally sound and fit for the use for which it 
was designed. The test program drawn up by the competent person should include every load-bearing 
part. The competent person should determine whether the original or if appropriate the previous test 
certificate is satisfactory, and subsequent testing should be based on this with any necessary

modifications to suit the requirements of the particular item of equipment. To prevent undue repetition, 
the test program should be conducted so that each load-bearing part is given one overload.  
 
Note: The competent person might wish to consult the manufacturer or other design authority to ensure 
that the selected test program is adequate. 
During testing with overloads all operations shall be carried out with extreme care and every permissible 
crane motion carried out singly at the lowest possible speed. Overloads shall be kept as close to the 
Platform/ground as possible, generally between 100 mm and 200 mm. It is preferred that overloads are 
not raised above 200 mm to allow them to pass over obstructions. Where such limitations exist the 
competent person should consider an alternative test to prove the crane in the restricted area. When no 
alternative test is possible and the competent person is not satisfied that all of the crane duties have 
been adequately tested, use of the crane should be restricted to the tested duties and both, the certificate 
of test and report of thorough examination endorsed accordingly.  To ensure that the load is applied at 
the correct position, any dimensions shall be checked using separate measuring equipment; the crane 
instrumentation shall not be used for this purpose. All measuring equipment should be accurate to ±1 % 
of the measured value unless specified otherwise and be undamaged and properly maintained. The 
weight of the lifting accessories should be included as part of the test load. During the test the 
performance of the crane should be monitored by the competent person. 
 
 
CRANES AND OTHER EQUIPMENT WHICH HAVE BEEN ALTERED OR REPAIRED 
In the event of any alteration or repair that could affect the stability of the crane, stability testing shall be 
carried out in accordance with the original crane specification. In the event of any alteration or repair 
which affects the strength of the crane, proof load testing shall be carried out to ensure that all parts 
affected by the repair are subjected to the test loads in accordance with the original crane specification. 
 
Note: It might be necessary to confirm with the manufacturer or other design authority the loads to be 
applied following such alterations or repairs. 
 
 
PROOF LOAD TEST CRITERIA 
Proof load testing of tower cranes shall be carried out after each erection, after reconfiguration (alteration 
of jib length, alteration of tower height, addition or removal of ties etc.), and for cranes remaining on the 
same site for long periods, at least once every 5 years. 
 
A visual examination of the crane and the test area shall be carried out prior to commencing the test. 
Before the application of a load a thorough examination of the crane shall be carried out, including 
verification that the crane is erected in accordance with the instructions. 
 
The competent person should check that the rated capacity specified on the manufacturer’s certificate of 
test, the load radius indicator, the table of rated capacities displayed in the operator’s cab and those used 
by the RCI/SLI. The rated capacities given should be identical. A functional test with no load applied shall 
be carried out to determine whether the controls, switches, contactors and other devices operate 
correctly. The adjustments of the brakes and limit switches shall be checked and tests carried out to 
ensure that they are operating correctly. 
 
Refer to Inspection Legislation Matrix in Appendix 12. 
 
Third Party Inspection Company to advice the required Action Plan for the proof load test and to be sent 
to ADNOC Group for review and approval prior to mobilize the Third Party Lifting Equipment Inspector 
to the site. Scope of proof load testing shall include inhibition of overload protection system (where fitted) 
to allow lifting of overload and verification of its proper adjustment and functionality after removal of 
inhibition. 
 
 
NON-DESTRUCTIVE TESTING (NDT) TECHNIQUES 
On completion of the tests, a thorough examination of the jib crane will be undertaken by the competent 
person to ensure no damage or permanent deformation has taken place, the competent person might

MACHINERY (AS APPLICABLE) 
 
 
 
 
 
 
FUNCTIONAL TEST 
Functional testing shall be carried out without a load applied. The object of functional testing is to 
determine whether the equipment performs as the manufacturer intended. This should include the 
operation of all controls to determine whether the equipment operates correctly and smoothly; and is free 
from wear and other damage. Functional testing shall be carried out on all functions of the forklift to check
